Unified agent · ✓ Pass 15s · 317,354 tok · $0.073 · 5 LLM / 7 tool calls · trace

**Fluorouracil (5-FU)** was the most common single treatment in MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024). It was recorded for **6,319 patients**. The cohort has 25,040 samples from 24,950 patients.

**Top agents by number of patients:**

| Rank | Agent | Category | Patients |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Fluorouracil | Chemo | 6,319 |
| 2 | Leucovorin | Chemo | 5,573 |
| 3 | Oxaliplatin | Chemo | 5,489 |
| 4 | Capecitabine | Chemo | 4,808 |
| 5 | Carboplatin | Chemo | 4,604 |
| 6 | Irinotecan | Chemo | 4,329 |
| 7 | Investigational agents | Investigational | 4,114 |
| 8 | Gemcitabine | Chemo | 4,038 |
| 9 | Leuprolide | Hormone | 3,708 |
| 10 | Paclitaxel | Chemo | 3,679 |

- **Chemotherapy dominates.** Nine of the top ten entries are chemotherapy or hormone agents, and the top eight are all chemo or investigational. Leuprolide (hormone therapy) is the only non-chemo agent in the top ten besides the pooled investigational group.
- **Regimens.** Counting same-day combinations, the most common regimen was single-agent **capecitabine** (4,434 patients). The most common multi-drug regimen was **fluorouracil + leucovorin + oxaliplatin** (2,974 patients), which is the FOLFOX combination.

**Caveats**
- These are raw patient counts, not percentages. The data can't separate "not treated" from "treatment not recorded," so percentages of the whole cohort would be misleading.
- Some treatment annotations come from natural language processing of clinical notes, so they may contain extraction errors.
- A patient can receive several agents, so the counts overlap and shouldn't be added together.

[Open the MSK-CHORD study in cBioPortal](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024) to see the treatment chart.
The answer correctly identifies Fluorouracil as the most common treatment received by patients in the MSK-CHORD study, matching the reference answer. The tool call results confirm 6,319 patients received Fluorouracil, the highest count among all agents. The link provided also matches the expected link.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 43s · 595,314 tok · $0.163 · 8 LLM / 11 tool calls · trace

In the Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025) study, the most frequently altered gene across mutations, copy number and SVs combined is **TP53**, altered in 38 of 158 samples (24.1%). The next four are tied at about 31 samples each.

I counted samples, not patients. A sample counts as altered if it has a mutation, a high-level amplification or a deep deletion in the gene. I used 158 as the denominator, which is every sample profiled for mutations or copy number. Individual genes may have been profiled in fewer samples, because only 143 samples have mutation data and 81 have copy-number data.

| Rank | Gene | Altered samples | % of 158 | Mutations | Amplifications | Deep deletions |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **TP53** | 38 | 24.1% | 32 | 1 | 5 |
| 2 | **LINC00901** | 32 | 20.3% | 0 | 0 | 32 |
| 3 (tie) | **CSMD3** | 31 | 19.6% | 6 | 26 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | **MYO15A** | 31 | 19.6% | 2 | 30 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | **RN7SL442P** | 31 | 19.6% | 0 | 31 | 0 |

- **Four-way tie:** CSMD3, MYO15A, RN7SL442P and TRIM16 each have 31 altered samples, so only three of the four fit in the top 5. I listed the first three alphabetically. TRIM16 (1 mutation, 29 amplifications, 1 deep deletion) is equally ranked.
- **No structural variants:** this study has no SV data, so the ranking reflects mutations and copy number only.
- **Copy-number events dominate:** apart from TP53, the top genes are mostly altered through amplification or deep deletion. Several are non-coding or pseudogene loci, which suggests broad copy-number segments rather than gene-specific events. TP53 is the only gene here with a substantial mutation count.
- **Alteration definition:** copy-number alterations are only amplifications and deep deletions. Gains and shallow deletions are not counted.

[View these genes in the OncoPrint](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=os_target_gdc&gene_list=TP53%0ALINC00901%0ACSMD3%0AMYO15A%0ARN7SL442P&case_set_id=os_target_gdc_all)

The portal's own percentages may differ slightly from mine, because it uses a different case set and denominator.

It explicitly notes there is no SV data in the study. The numbers match the reference exactly, and the answer satisfies all required notes.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 11s · 325,725 tok · $0.075 · 5 LLM / 6 tool calls · trace

The median age at diagnosis for osteosarcoma patients in the TARGET GDC study is **about 15.2 years**.

This comes from 293 patients who have a recorded age. The interquartile range is 12.2–17.7 years, and the full range is 3.6–87.1 years.

- **Study:** Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025), `os_target_gdc`. It's the only TARGET osteosarcoma study. I counted patients, not samples.
- **Where the age comes from:** I calculated it from the recorded days from birth to diagnosis. The study's standard Age field is floored at 18 for every patient under 18, which is 241 of the 293. A median taken from that field would wrongly come out as 18.
- **Missing data:** 90 of the study's 383 patients with clinical data have no age recorded, so they are not in this figure.

**52.5% of patients in the MSK-CHORD study have at least one TP53 mutation.** That is 13,105 of 24,950 patients profiled for TP53.

- Almost every patient has one sample. At the sample level, 13,124 of 25,040 samples carry a TP53 mutation, which is 52.4%.
- The count includes every called somatic or unknown-status TP53 mutation within the sequenced gene panel. It excludes uncalled variants. It makes no judgement about whether a mutation is a driver.
- The study covers five cancer types: non-small cell lung, colorectal, breast, prostate and pancreatic. The 52.5% is a pooled figure across all five, so it will differ within each type.

**Links**
- [MSK-CHORD study view, filtered to TP53-mutated cases](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024#filterJson=%7B%22geneFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22geneQueries%22%3A%5B%5B%7B%22hugoGeneSymbol%22%3A%22TP53%22%7D%5D%5D%2C%22molecularProfileIds%22%3A%5B%22msk_chord_2024_mutations%22%5D%7D%5D%7D) — cohort overview, clinical breakdowns and counts for the TP53-mutated group.

I can't tell you from the database whether the two groups differ in overall survival. That needs a Kaplan-Meier curve and a log-rank test, which I can't run. I've set up that comparison in cBioPortal for you (link below).

**Cohort:** breast cancer patients in [MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024). I counted patients, not samples. A patient is PIK3CA-mutated if any of their samples carries a PIK3CA mutation. A wild-type patient had a sample profiled for PIK3CA and no mutation in it.

| Group | Patients | Deaths | Censored (alive at last follow-up) | Follow-up range (months) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PIK3CA-mutated | 1,908 | 718 | 1,190 | 0.03 – 113.1 |
| PIK3CA wild-type | 3,460 | 1,272 | 2,188 | 0.03 – 118.5 |

All 5,368 breast cancer patients in the study have overall survival data, and all were profiled for PIK3CA.

- **Censoring:** Most patients in both groups were still alive at last follow-up (62% of mutated, 63% of wild-type). Raw averages or medians of follow-up time would therefore be misleading. I haven't reported them.
- **Median survival:** Fewer than half of the patients in each group have died. The Kaplan-Meier median is therefore likely not reached in either group, or at least not well estimated.
- **Significance and effect size:** I haven't computed a p-value or hazard ratio. The death counts above don't show whether survival differs.

**Next step:** [PIK3CA mutated vs wild-type: Kaplan-Meier survival comparison](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/survival?comparisonId=6b06d7688edee9e215916abd) shows the survival curves and the log-rank p-value. #23 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · msk_chord_2024What is the correlation between tumor mutational burden and microsatellite instability status in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD Study? ✓29s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Tumor mutational burden (TMB) demonstrates a significant positive association with microsatellite instability (MSI) in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD cohort. Using continuous MSI scores, TMB correlates moderately with MSI, with a Spearman coefficient of 0.37 (p = 1.18 × 10⁻¹⁷⁵) and a Pearson coefficient of 0.58 (p = 0.00), indicating that higher MSI scores correspond to higher mutational burden. Consistent with these quantitative correlations, categorical MSI typing shows a clear separation wherein MSI-instable tumors exhibit high TMB, MSI-stable tumors show uniformly low TMB, and indeterminate cases fall between these groups.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 29s · 445,101 tok · $0.145 · 6 LLM / 11 tool calls · trace

TMB and MSI status are positively correlated in colorectal cancer in MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024). MSI-instable tumors have a much higher TMB than MSI-stable ones. I counted samples, and the cohort is nearly one sample per patient. TMB is the nonsynonymous mutations per Mb.

**TMB by MSI status (colorectal cancer samples)**

| MSI status | Samples | Mean TMB | Median TMB | IQR (Q1–Q3) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stable | 4,682 | 7.58 | 5.87 | 4.32–7.78 |
| Instable | 586 | 64.63 | 56.68 | 44.10–75.94 |
| Indeterminate | 188 | 18.57 | 6.56 | 4.92–10.15 |
| Do not report | 49 | 7.06 | 3.33 | 0.86–6.92 |
| Not recorded | 38 | 8.06 | 3.69 | 1.64–5.74 |

**Correlation coefficients**

| Comparison | n | Coefficient |
|---|---|---|
| TMB vs MSI class (Stable vs Instable only) | 5,268 | Point-biserial r = 0.60; Spearman ρ = 0.64 |
| TMB vs continuous MSI score | 5,505 | Pearson r = 0.58; Spearman ρ = 0.37 |

- **Group separation:** The median TMB in the instable group (about 57) is roughly 10 times that of the stable group (about 6). The two groups' interquartile ranges do not overlap.
- **Weaker rank correlation with the MSI score:** The Spearman ρ of 0.37 is lower than the Pearson r of 0.58. The MSI score is probably not linear with TMB across its range. Extreme TMB values in a few hypermutated samples may also inflate the Pearson value.
- **TMB of 10 or more:** 582 of 586 instable samples (99%) reach this level. So do 357 of 4,682 stable samples (8%).
- **Hypermutated stable tumors:** Some MSI-stable tumors have very high TMB (maximum about 571). These are likely hypermutated for reasons other than MSI. That is my reading, not something the data states.
- **Sample counts:** The correlations use all colorectal samples with both values. Because a few patients have more than one sample, patient and sample counts differ slightly.

I haven't run a significance test, so I'm not reporting p-values. cBioPortal's Group Comparison runs the appropriate tests. For TMB, a numeric clinical attribute, the default is a Wilcoxon rank-sum test. You can also run `scipy.stats.spearmanr` or R's `cor.test(method="spearman")` on the per-sample values.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 42s · 702,810 tok · $0.233 · 8 LLM / 15 tool calls · trace

**A same-patient comparison isn't possible in MSK-CHORD.** The study has almost no patients with more than one sample.

- It has 24,950 patients and 25,040 samples.
- Only 90 patients have more than one sample.
- Only 26 patients have both a Primary and a Metastasis sample.
- In all 26 of those patients, the two samples come from different cancer types. For example, one patient has a breast primary and a lung metastasis. These look like second primaries, not a primary and its own metastasis, so they can't serve as a paired set.

I ran the **unpaired** comparison instead: all Primary samples against all Metastasis samples. Different patients contribute to each group.

## Unpaired comparison (sample level)

Cohort: [MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024). It has 15,928 Primary and 8,878 Metastasis samples. The 136 Unknown and 98 Local Recurrence samples are excluded.

Percentages are altered samples divided by samples profiled for that gene. Each gene's profiled count is panel-aware, so it is 15,928 and 8,878 for most genes.

| Gene | Primary | Metastasis | Difference (Met − Primary, percentage points) |
|---|---|---|---|
| TP53 | 8,104/15,928 (50.9%) | 4,890/8,878 (55.1%) | +4.2 |
| KRAS | 4,751 (29.8%) | 2,326 (26.2%) | −3.6 |
| APC | 3,345 (21.0%) | 1,400 (15.8%) | −5.2 |
| PIK3CA | 2,351 (14.8%) | 1,327 (14.9%) | +0.1 |
| EGFR | 1,338 (8.4%) | 779 (8.8%) | +0.4 |
| ARID1A | 1,199 (7.5%) | 634 (7.1%) | −0.4 |
| SMAD4 | 1,142 (7.2%) | 643 (7.2%) | 0.0 |
| KMT2D | 1,203 (7.6%) | 569 (6.4%) | −1.2 |
| FBXW7 | 876 (5.5%) | 238 (2.7%) | −2.8 |
| BRAF | 826 (5.2%) | 307 (3.5%) | −1.7 |
| KEAP1 | 702 (4.4%) | 475 (5.4%) | +1.0 |
| GATA3 | 634 (4.0%) | 458 (5.2%) | +1.2 |
| CDH1 | 554 (3.5%) | 406 (4.6%) | +1.1 |

The table shows 13 of the 25 most frequently mutated genes. The rest (KMT2C, ATM, CDKN2A, FAT1, ZFHX3, NF1, PTEN, STK11, PTPRT, SMARCA4, PTPRD, RBM10) differ by less than 1 percentage point, except ZFHX3 and RBM10, which differ by about 1.

**What the counts show**
- Most of the common genes have similar frequencies in the two groups.
- TP53 is higher in metastases, by about 4 points.
- APC, KRAS, FBXW7 and BRAF are lower in metastases.
- KEAP1, GATA3 and CDH1 are slightly higher in metastases.
- TMB is similar. Nonsynonymous TMB has a median of 4.1 in primaries and 4.32 in metastases. The means are 7.77 and 6.35, so the distribution is skewed.

**Cancer-type mix is a confounder.** MSK-CHORD has only five cancer types, and the two groups contain them in different proportions:

| Cancer type | Primary | Metastasis |
|---|---|---|
| Non-small cell lung | 4,908 | 2,763 |
| Colorectal | 4,054 | 1,457 |
| Breast | 2,859 | 2,464 |
| Prostate | 2,130 | 1,070 |
| Pancreatic | 1,977 | 1,124 |

Breast cancer makes up a larger share of the metastasis group, and colorectal cancer a smaller share. The lower APC rate and higher GATA3 and CDH1 rates in metastases may therefore reflect this mix and not a primary-versus-metastasis effect. A cancer-type-stratified comparison would address that.

I can't compute significance here, and I haven't made any claim about it. The comparison link below runs the enrichment tests with multiple-testing correction. For a stricter analysis, you could stratify by cancer type, for example by adding a cancer-type filter to both groups.

**About 1.03% of genomic events in MSK-CHORD are off-panel.** That is 2,877 of 279,290 events. I counted events (individual mutation, copy-number and structural-variant calls), not samples or patients. The cohort is 25,040 samples. No mutations in this study carry the uncalled status.

| Event type | Total events | Off-panel | % off-panel |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mutations | 208,232 | 150 | 0.07% |
| Copy-number alterations (amplifications and deep deletions) | 60,787 | 0 | 0% |
| Structural variants (fusions) | 10,271 | 2,727 | 26.55% |
| **All events** | **279,290** | **2,877** | **1.03%** |

- **Structural variants account for most of the off-panel events.** 2,727 of the 2,877 off-panel events (about 95%) are structural variants. Roughly a quarter of all structural variant calls involve a gene outside the sequencing panel used for that sample.
- **Copy-number events are never off-panel.** None of the 60,787 copy-number events are flagged off-panel.
- **Mutations are almost always on-panel.** Only 150 of 208,232 are off-panel.
- **The 1.03% depends on how many events of each type exist.** It is low mainly because mutations and copy-number events greatly outnumber structural variants.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 29s · 473,724 tok · $0.159 · 6 LLM / 9 tool calls · trace

I can't say whether these alterations are mutually exclusive. That needs a statistical test (Fisher's exact test with an odds ratio), which I can't run. Below are the counts for the TCGA PanCancer Atlas GBM cohort, plus a link to the portal's test.

**Mutations only (patients; 390 profiled)**

| Gene | Patients mutated |
|---|---|
| CDKN2A | 4 |
| CDK4 | 0 |
| RB1 | 38 |

| Pair | Patients with both |
|---|---|
| CDKN2A + CDK4 | 0 |
| CDKN2A + RB1 | 1 |
| CDK4 + RB1 | 0 |
| All three | 0 |

A total of 41 patients (10.5%) have a mutation in at least one of the three genes. CDK4 has no mutations in this cohort, so no pair involving it can be tested. With only 4 CDKN2A mutants, the other two pairs have very little power.

**Mutations plus copy-number events (AMP or HOMDEL; samples; 378 profiled for both mutations and CNA)**

In GBM, these genes are mostly altered by copy-number change, so mutation-only counts understate the picture.

| Gene | Samples altered |
|---|---|
| CDKN2A (mostly homozygous deletion) | 217 (57.4%) |
| CDK4 (mostly amplification) | 60 (15.9%) |
| RB1 | 47 (12.4%) |

| Pair | Samples with both |
|---|---|
| CDKN2A + CDK4 | 13 |
| CDKN2A + RB1 | 7 |
| CDK4 + RB1 | 0 |
| All three | 0 |

In total, 304 samples (80.4%) have at least one alteration in these genes. The mutation-plus-CNA counts are at sample level, and the mutation-only counts are at patient level.

**How to test it**

The Mutual Exclusivity tab runs the statistics for every gene pair and reports an odds ratio, p-value and q-value. [Open the Mutual Exclusivity tab for CDKN2A, CDK4 and RB1 (mutations only)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutualExclusivity?cancer_study_list=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=CDKN2A%3A+MUT%0ACDK4%3A+MUT%0ARB1%3A+MUT&case_set_id=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all)

That link counts mutations only. To include deletions and amplifications, edit the gene list on the portal to plain symbols (`CDKN2A`, `CDK4`, `RB1`).

You can also run Fisher's exact test yourself, for example in R with `fisher.test()` or in Python with `scipy.stats.fisher_exact`. The 2×2 table for any pair is (both, A only, B only, neither), using the counts above.
The answer correctly identifies that CDKN2A and CDK4 alterations in GBM are predominantly copy-number events (homozygous deletion and amplification) rather than point mutations, presents the co-occurrence counts from the tool results (CDK4+RB1=0, CDKN2A+RB1=7, CDKN2A+CDK4=13), describes the tendency toward mutual exclusivity, and directs the user to the Mutual Exclusivity tab for statistical testing without inventing p-values or log odds ratios. - **R:** `t.test(log2(cdk4_amp + 1), log2(cdk4_diploid + 1), var.equal = FALSE)`
- **Python:** `scipy.stats.ttest_ind(a, b, equal_var=False)`

| Group | n | Mean log2 | SD | Median log2 | IQR (Q1–Q3) | Median raw RSEM |

- **Non-truncating mutants:** missense and in-frame mutants have slightly higher expression than wild-type. This fits the idea that truncating mutations trigger transcript loss while missense mutations don't. That interpretation is general knowledge, not something I read from this data.

**Testing significance:** for two groups of continuous expression data, cBioPortal's default is a Student's t-test on log2-transformed values. In R, the test would be `t.test(log2(rsem+1) ~ group)`, after pulling the group values from the portal. In TCGA ovarian cancer, higher BRCA1 methylation goes with lower BRCA1 mRNA. The correlation is moderate, and the effect comes mostly from a small group of tumors with high methylation.

**Cohort and data:** I used the TCGA ovarian serous cystadenocarcinoma cohorts (Firehose Legacy and Nature 2011). Methylation comes from the HumanMethylation27 array (beta values, 0 to 1), which has the BRCA1 promoter probe. I matched methylation to mRNA for each sample. The unit is samples, and each sample is a separate patient tumor.

**Spearman correlation between BRCA1 methylation and mRNA** (a negative value means more methylation, less mRNA)

| Cohort | mRNA data | Paired samples | Spearman ρ |
|---|---|---|---|
| TCGA Firehose Legacy | Agilent array | 550 | −0.40 |
| TCGA Firehose Legacy | RNA-seq V2 | 298 | −0.44 |
| TCGA Nature 2011 | Agilent array | 489 | −0.36 |

**BRCA1 mRNA by methylation level** (Firehose Legacy, array mRNA z-scores, n = 550)

| BRCA1 methylation group | Samples | Mean z-score | Median z-score |
|---|---|---|---|
| Low (beta < 0.1) | 446 | −0.11 | −0.08 |
| Intermediate (0.1–0.3) | 32 | −0.50 | −0.64 |
| High (beta ≥ 0.3) | 72 | −2.07 | −2.18 |

- **Direction:** All three comparisons show the same negative relationship, and it holds across both mRNA platforms and both cohorts.
- **High methylation:** Tumors with high methylation (72 of 550, about 13%) have BRCA1 mRNA about 2 standard deviations below the cohort mean. That is a large shift.
- **Low methylation:** Most tumors have little methylation, and their mRNA sits around the cohort average.
- **Why ρ is only moderate:** The relationship is not a smooth gradient. It is mostly a threshold effect in the small, heavily methylated group. Many unmethylated tumors also have low BRCA1 mRNA, which suggests other mechanisms (for example copy-number loss or mutation).

**Statistical testing:** I did not run a significance test. The group comparison above would normally use a t-test or ANOVA. You can run it in the cBioPortal plot linked below, which reports the correlation and group tests. In R, `t.test` or `cor.test(method="spearman")` also work.

**Caveats:** These are bulk-tumor measurements, and the HM27 array covers only a limited set of CpG sites. The data show association, not causation.

**View in cBioPortal:** [BRCA1 methylation vs. mRNA expression plot (TCGA Ovarian, Firehose Legacy)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/plots?cancer_study_list=ov_tcga&gene_list=BRCA1&case_set_id=ov_tcga_all&plots_horz_selection=%7B%22selectedGeneOption%22%3A%22672%22%2C%22dataType%22%3A%22METHYLATION%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22methylation_hm27%22%7D&plots_vert_selection=%7B%22selectedGeneOption%22%3A%22672%22%2C%22dataType%22%3A%22MRNA_EXPRESSION%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22mrna_median_Zscores%22%7D)

#39 AnalysisSurvival & outcomes · luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018"What are the survival differences between EGFR-mutated, EGFR-amplified, and EGFR wild-type lung adenocarcinoma patients?" ✓31s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-24)
“Across patients from the Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, EGFR mutations were not associated with a statistically significant difference in overall survival compared with EGFR–wild-type tumors (log-rank p = 0.125). Median overall survival was 42.5 months for EGFR-mutant patients versus 53.3 months for wild-type patients, with an estimated hazard ratio (HR) of 1.38 (95% CI: 0.87–2.18), indicating no clear survival disadvantage. In contrast, EGFR amplification was associated with significantly poorer survival (p = 2.82 × 10⁻³). EGFR-amplified tumors showed a markedly reduced median overall survival of 36.7 months compared with 53.3 months for EGFR–wild-type patients. The hazard ratio for amplified tumors was 2.15 (95% CI: 1.05–4.41), consistent with substantially shorter survival in this subgroup. Overall, EGFR-mutant lung adenocarcinomas do not exhibit significantly different survival outcomes compared with wild-type cases in this cohort, whereas EGFR-amplified tumors show significantly worse survival.”

| | NRAS | F78S | 40 / 148 | 0.21 |
| TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 | KRAS | K176Q | 137 / 187 | 0.42 |
| | NRAS | E162* | 19 / 168 | 0.10 |
| TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 | KRAS | G12D | 68 / 264 | 0.21 |
| | NRAS | Q61K | 43 / 183 | 0.19 |
| TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 | KRAS | A146V | 14 / 51 | 0.22 |
| | NRAS | Q61R | 14 / 42 | 0.25 |

**What the VAFs suggest**
- **Likely clonal:** KRAS Q61H (0.52) and KRAS K176Q (0.42) are at or near 0.5. That fits a heterozygous mutation present in most tumor cells, though copy-number changes could also produce it.
- **Lower VAF, 0.19–0.29:** Seven of the nine mutations sit here. These could be subclonal, or clonal mutations in a lower-purity tumor.
- **Best subclonal candidate:** NRAS E162* (0.10) is the lowest VAF, and it sits beside a KRAS mutation at 0.42 in the same sample.
- **Within-sample comparisons:**
  - In A0G2 and AX-A0J0, the KRAS mutation has a higher VAF than the NRAS mutation(s). That is consistent with KRAS arising earlier, or in a larger clone, than NRAS.
  - In B5-A0JV (0.21 vs 0.19) and DF-A2KZ (0.22 vs 0.25), the two genes have similar VAFs. They may sit in the same clone.

**Caveats**
- **Raw VAFs only:** The database has no cancer cell fraction (CCF) estimates for this study. The VAFs aren't adjusted for tumor purity or local copy number, so the clonal/subclonal labels above are suggestions only.
- **Low read depth:** DF-A2KZ has only 14 alt reads per mutation, so its VAFs are imprecise.
- **No statistics:** With 4 samples and 9 mutations, I haven't tested anything.
- **Not checked:** I haven't checked whether these 4 tumors are hypermutated (for example, the POLE or MSI subtypes).

[View KRAS/NRAS mutations in these 4 samples (Mutations tab)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?session_id=6b06d818c53d845ad81d97b7)

[View the 4-sample cohort in Study View](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22sampleIdentifiers%22%3A%5B%7B%22studyId%22%3A%22ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%22%2C%22sampleId%22%3A%22TCGA-A5-A0G2-01%22%7D%2C%7B%22studyId%22%3A%22ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%22%2C%22sampleId%22%3A%22TCGA-AX-A0J0-01%22%7D%2C%7B%22studyId%22%3A%22ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%22%2C%22sampleId%22%3A%22TCGA-B5-A0JV-01%22%7D%2C%7B%22studyId%22%3A%22ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%22%2C%22sampleId%22%3A%22TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01%22%7D%5D%7D)

The Mutations tab shows the allele counts per mutation.
The answer correctly identifies all 4 samples, reports the correct VAFs matching the reference (TCGA-A5-A0G2: KRAS Q61H 0.52, NRAS D47N 0.29, F78S 0.21; TCGA-AX-A0J0: KRAS K176Q 0.42, NRAS E162* 0.10; TCGA-B5-A0JV: KRAS G12D 0.21, NRAS Q61K 0.19; TCGA-DF-A2KZ: KRAS A146V 0.22, NRAS Q61R 0.25), reaches the same conclusions as the reference about lower NRAS VAFs in the first two samples suggesting subclonal events and similar VAFs in the last two, and explicitly caveats that raw VAFs are not adjusted for purity/copy number, satisfying the 'must not overinterpret clonality' requirement.
#44 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018What percentage of endometrial cancer samples have hypermutation (>5000 mutations) and how does this correlate with histological subtype? ✓33s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
According to the Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, approximately 7–8% of endometrial cancer samples are hypermutated (>5000 mutations). These hypermutated tumors show strong enrichment for the POLE subtype and, to a lesser extent, MSI tumors, whereas non-hypermutated tumors are primarily CN-high or CN-low.
Expected links
https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/clinical?comparisonId=692051a8b2bb32147b014312

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 33s · 504,640 tok · $0.188 · 6 LLM / 11 tool calls · trace

**In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas endometrial cohort, 38 of 517 samples (7.4%) have more than 5,000 mutations.** The cohort has 529 samples in total, and 12 of them have no mutation count recorded. I used this study because it is the standard TCGA reference cohort, and I counted samples. The numbers below come from the study's per-sample mutation count.

**By histology**

| Histology | Samples with a count | >5,000 mutations | % |
|---|---|---|---|
| Uterine Endometrioid Carcinoma | 388 | 34 | 8.8% |
| Uterine Mixed Endometrial Carcinoma | 21 | 2 | 9.5% |
| Uterine Serous / Papillary Serous Carcinoma | 108 | 2 | 1.9% |
| **All** | **517** | **38** | **7.4%** |

- **Endometrioid:** 34 of the 38 hypermutated samples (89%) are endometrioid. The mixed group is too small (n=21) to compare reliably.
- **Serous:** hypermutation is rare, with only 2 of 108 samples. The median mutation count is 54, against 142 for endometrioid.

**By TCGA molecular subtype**

Histology is a weak proxy here. The molecular subtype explains the hypermutation much better.

| Subtype | Samples | >5,000 mutations | Median mutation count | Max |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| POLE | 49 | 32 (65%) | 7,385 | 25,697 |
| MSI | 148 | 6 (4%) | 578 | 11,439 |
| CN-low | 147 | 0 | 50 | 1,508 |
| CN-high | 163 | 0 | 54 | 353 |
| Not classified | 10 | 0 | 49 | 493 |

- **POLE-mutant tumors:** they make up 32 of the 38 hypermutated samples (84%).
- **MSI tumors:** they have high mutation counts, but few exceed 5,000. Most of the 6 that do are endometrioid.
- **Serous histology:** its 2 hypermutated samples are both POLE subtype. The other 97 or so serous samples are mostly CN-high, and none of those is hypermutated.
- **Hypermutated endometrioid samples:** 29 are POLE and 5 are MSI.
- **Mixed histology:** 1 hypermutated sample is POLE and 1 is MSI.

**Caveats**
- A 5,000-mutation cutoff is very strict. Many MSI tumors have high counts that fall below it. Using a cutoff of 1,000, 77 samples qualify (14.9%).
- I have not run a statistical test for histology against hypermutation. These are descriptive counts only. The group comparison link below lets you test the groups in the portal.
- Hypermutation is concentrated in endometrioid tumors, but this comes mainly from the POLE subtype. It is not a histology effect on its own.

#45 AnalysisAlteration frequency · ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018What are the most frequently mutated genes in copy-number high subtype endometrial cancers compared to other subtypes? ✓44s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
In copy-number-high (serous-like) endometrial cancers (163 patients) vs other subtypes (344): TP53 87% vs 15%, PPP2R1A 31% vs 10%, PIK3CA 33% vs 58%, FBXW7 18% vs 19%, while PTEN (17% vs 88%) and ARID1A are much less frequent in CN-high.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 44s · 741,282 tok · $0.224 · 9 LLM / 13 tool calls · trace

In TCGA endometrial cancer, TP53 is by far the most frequently mutated gene in the copy-number high subtype. Mutations in PPP2R1A are also much more common there than in the other subtypes.

I used the TCGA PanCancer Atlas uterine corpus endometrial carcinoma study. The counts are patients with a somatic mutation, out of patients profiled for mutations and assigned a molecular subtype. That is 163 copy-number high patients and 344 in the other subtypes combined (copy-number low 147, MSI 148, POLE 49). Patients with no subtype assigned are excluded. The table shows the top 15 genes in the copy-number high group.

| Gene | CN-high (n=163) | Other subtypes (n=344) |
|---|---|---|
| **TP53** | 141 (86.5%) | 50 (14.5%) |
| **PIK3CA** | 54 (33.1%) | 200 (58.1%) |
| **PPP2R1A** | 50 (30.7%) | 35 (10.2%) |
| TTN | 32 (19.6%) | 171 (49.7%) |
| FBXW7 | 30 (18.4%) | 64 (18.6%) |
| PTEN | 28 (17.2%) | 303 (88.1%) |
| CHD4 | 25 (15.3%) | 88 (25.6%) |
| PIK3R1 | 23 (14.1%) | 134 (39.0%) |
| CSMD3 | 20 (12.3%) | 101 (29.4%) |
| ARHGAP35 | 18 (11.0%) | 85 (24.7%) |
| MUC16 | 17 (10.4%) | 125 (36.3%) |
| RYR2 | 13 (8.0%) | 112 (32.6%) |
| SPOP | 13 (8.0%) | 42 (12.2%) |
| TAF1 | 12 (7.4%) | 92 (26.7%) |
| DMD | 12 (7.4%) | 89 (25.9%) |

**Higher in copy-number high**
- **TP53:** 86.5% in copy-number high, against 6.1% in copy-number low, 16.2% in MSI and 34.7% in POLE.
- **PPP2R1A:** 30.7% in copy-number high, against 2.0% in copy-number low, 12.2% in MSI and 28.6% in POLE.

**Lower in copy-number high**
- **PTEN:** 17.2% in copy-number high, against 80.3% in copy-number low, 93.2% in MSI and 95.9% in POLE.
- **PIK3CA:** 33.1% in copy-number high, against 50.3% in copy-number low, 57.4% in MSI and 83.7% in POLE.
- **PIK3R1:** lower in copy-number high (14.1% against 39.0%).
- **Large genes:** TTN, MUC16, RYR2 and DMD are much less often mutated in copy-number high. This probably reflects the high mutation burden of the MSI and POLE subtypes, not subtype-specific biology.

**Caveats**
- These are descriptive frequencies only. I did not run a significance test, so I can't say which differences are statistically significant. The Group Comparison link below runs the tests in cBioPortal, with Fisher's exact test and multiple-testing correction.
- "Frequently mutated" does not mean driver. No driver annotation is stored for this study.
- The "other subtypes" column pools three very different groups. The POLE and MSI subtypes are hypermutated, so the per-subtype percentages in the bullets are more informative than the pooled column.

Thyroid carcinoma and melanoma have by far the highest BRAF V600E frequencies across the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies. Colorectal adenocarcinoma is a distant third.

| Rank | Cancer type | V600E samples | Profiled samples | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Thyroid Carcinoma (THCA) | 284 | 490 | **58.0%** |
| 2 | Skin Cutaneous Melanoma (SKCM) | 158 | 440 | **35.9%** |
| 3 | Colorectal Adenocarcinoma (COADREAD) | 48 | 534 | **9.0%** |
| 4 | Cholangiocarcinoma (CHOL) | 1 | 36 | 2.8% |
| 5 | Lung Adenocarcinoma (LUAD) | 9 | 566 | 1.6% |
| 6 | Glioblastoma (GBM) | 5 | 397 | 1.3% |

- **Rare cases:** V600E is also seen in KIRP (2/276), LGG (1/514), BLCA (1/410) and HNSC (1/515). Each is 0.7% or lower.
- **Cholangiocarcinoma:** the 2.8% rests on a single case in a small cohort of 36, so it is not a stable estimate.
- **No V600E calls:** the other 20 studies had none. That includes UVM, LUSC, BRCA, OV and STAD.
- **Counting unit:** these are sample-level counts. I counted only the exact V600E protein change and excluded other BRAF mutations such as V600K.
- **Overlap:** each study is a separate TCGA cohort, so the studies are not combined. No pan-cancer total was computed.

ERBB2 mRNA is expressed in almost every TCGA Pan-Cancer Atlas cancer type, and its level varies by about 6 log2 units between the highest and lowest groups. Breast cancer has the highest upper tail, because a subset of tumors expresses very high levels.

[View ERBB2 mRNA expression by cancer type (Plots tab)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/plots?cancer_study_list=acc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cblca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cbrca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccesc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cchol_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccoadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cdlbc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cesca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cgbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Chnsc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckich_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirp_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Claml_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clihc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cluad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cmeso_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cov_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpaad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpcpg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cprad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Csarc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cskcm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cstad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ctgct_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthym_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cuvm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=ERBB2&case_set_id=all&plots_horz_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22CANCER_TYPE%22%7D&plots_vert_selection=%7B%22selectedGeneOption%22%3A%222064%22%2C%22dataType%22%3A%22MRNA_EXPRESSION%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22rna_seq_v2_mrna%22%2C%22logScale%22%3A%22true%22%7D)

**How I measured it.** The cohort is the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies (samples with ERBB2 RNA-seq data), grouped by cancer type. I used batch-normalized RSEM values, shown as log2(RSEM+1). That scale is comparable across these studies. I did not use z-scores, because each study is centered on its own reference and that hides differences between cancer types.

**Main patterns**
- **Highest median:** Bladder (12.88), breast (12.78), thyroid (12.71) and non-clear-cell renal carcinoma (12.63). Most epithelial tumors have medians of about 12 to 12.8.
- **Breast has the highest upper tail:** Its 90th percentile (14.75) is well above its median (12.78). Bladder (14.28) and esophagogastric cancer (13.75) also have long upper tails. A subset of tumors in these types expresses ERBB2 far above the typical level. I did not test what drives that subset, for example amplification.
- **Lowest expression:** Hematologic and neural-crest or neuroendocrine tumors have the lowest levels. These are leukemia (median 6.14), pheochromocytoma (7.75), mature B-cell neoplasms (7.87) and adrenocortical carcinoma (7.38). Glioma (9.32) is also low.
- **Intermediate:** Melanoma, sarcoma, glioblastoma, seminoma, and thymic and ocular tumors have medians of about 9.6 to 10.7.

The plot link shows the full per-sample distributions, so you can see the high-expressing outliers in each cancer type.

**Caveats:**
- These are descriptive summaries. I ran no statistical tests between cancer types.
- Samples are counted, not patients.
- Cancer type here is the portal's `CANCER_TYPE` grouping, which merges some studies. Lung adenocarcinoma and squamous carcinoma are combined as Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer. Colon and rectal are combined as Colorectal Cancer.
- The tumors in each category come from separate TCGA studies, so sample counts are not a direct measure of prevalence.

**ERBB2 in TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer: how often amplification, mRNA overexpression and protein overexpression agree**

I used three definitions. Amplification is a GISTIC call of +2. mRNA overexpression is a z-score above 2 (RNA-seq V2, all-sample reference). Protein overexpression is an RPPA z-score above 2.

The three-way comparison uses the 863 samples that have all three data types. The two-way comparisons use every sample profiled for that pair.

**Three-way concordance (863 samples)**

| Amplified | mRNA high | Protein high | Samples |
|---|---|---|---|
| Yes | Yes | Yes | 42 |
| Yes | Yes | No | 16 |
| Yes | No | Yes | 8 |
| Yes | No | No | 35 |
| No | Yes | Yes | 2 |
| No | Yes | No | 2 |
| No | No | No | 758 |

- **Amplified samples:** 101 of the 863 are amplified. 42 of them (42%) are high on all three measures. Another 24 (24%) are high on one of the other two, and 35 (35%) are amplified with neither mRNA nor protein above the threshold.
- **Non-amplified samples:** 762 are not amplified. 758 of them (99.5%) are also negative for mRNA and protein overexpression. Only 4 show mRNA overexpression without amplification.
- **No protein-only cases:** No sample is high for protein without also being amplified or high for mRNA.

**Pairwise concordance**

| Comparison | Result |
|---|---|
| Amplification and mRNA | 70 of 122 amplified samples (57%) have high mRNA. Only 4 of the 946 non-amplified samples (0.4%) do. |
| Amplification and protein | 50 of 102 amplified samples (49%) have high protein. Only 2 of the 775 non-amplified samples (0.3%) do. |
| mRNA and protein | 45 of 63 mRNA-high samples (71%) have high protein. 8 of 811 mRNA-normal samples (1%) do. The Spearman correlation across all samples is about 0.53. |

**Interpretation**
- **Specificity:** Overexpression of mRNA or protein almost always comes with amplification, so specificity is very high.
- **Sensitivity:** Sensitivity is lower. About half of amplified samples fall below a z > 2 cutoff.
- **Likely reasons for the gap:** Low-level amplification is one possibility. Tumor purity and the z-score cutoff may also play a part. Amplified samples have a median mRNA z-score of 2.31, so many sit near the threshold. I haven't tested these explanations against the data.
- **Protein data:** Protein z-scores come from RPPA, which covers 876 of the 1,084 samples. All of the comparisons above are restricted to samples with data for the measures being compared. The separate mass-spec protein profile covers only 105 samples, so I didn't use it.
- **Statistics:** I haven't run any significance tests on these comparisons. The cBioPortal Plots and Group Comparison tabs can do that.

#49 AnalysisExpression & multi-omics · All TCGA PancanWhich cancer types have the highest aneuploidy scores and how does this correlate with mutation burden across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies? ✓49s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Across TCGA Pan-Cancer studies, tumors with the highest aneuploidy burdens included seminoma, non-seminomatous germ cell tumor, adrenocortical carcinoma, non–small cell lung cancer, and bladder cancer, all of which displayed elevated median aneuploidy scores. Although bladder cancer and non–small cell lung cancer also ranked among the tumor types with the highest mutational burdens, seminoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors showed some of the lowest TMB values, with adrenocortical carcinoma exhibiting intermediate levels. These patterns indicate that aneuploidy does not reliably coincide with high mutation loads across tumor types.

# Aneuploidy score vs. mutation burden across TCGA PanCancer Atlas

I used 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohorts, with 9,975 samples that have both an aneuploidy score and a TMB value. TMB is the nonsynonymous tumor mutational burden in mutations per Mb. The counting unit is samples.

## Cancer types ranked by aneuploidy score

| Rank | Cancer type | n | Median aneuploidy | Mean aneuploidy | Median TMB | Mean TMB |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Uterine Carcinosarcoma (UCS) | 56 | 19 | 17.2 | 1.55 | 4.32 |
| 2 | Testicular Germ Cell (TGCT) | 149 | 19 | 18.8 | 0.40 | 0.49 |
| 3 | Adrenocortical (ACC) | 89 | 17 | 18.3 | 0.90 | 2.83 |
| 4 | Lung Adenocarcinoma (LUAD) | 501 | 16 | 15.0 | 6.87 | 10.24 |
| 5 | Lung Squamous (LUSC) | 479 | 16 | 16.2 | 7.73 | 9.51 |
| 6 | Esophageal (ESCA) | 162 | 14.5 | 14.6 | 3.65 | 5.08 |
| 7 | Kidney Chromophobe (KICH) | 65 | 14 | 14.9 | 0.67 | 1.09 |
| 8 | Bladder (BLCA) | 400 | 14 | 13.6 | 5.90 | 8.57 |
| 9 | Ovarian (OV) | 504 | 13 | 14.0 | 1.90 | 2.26 |
| 10 | Colorectal (COADREAD) | 529 | 12 | 12.3 | 3.50 | 13.02 |

**Lowest aneuploidy (median):**
- Thyroid (THCA), AML (LAML) and Thymoma (THYM) each have a median of 0.
- Prostate (PRAD) has a median of 1.
- Lower-grade glioma (LGG) has a median of 2.
- Uterine endometrial (UCEC) has a median of 3.

**Highest TMB (median):** SKCM 15.3, LUSC 7.7, LUAD 6.9 and BLCA 5.9. UCEC has the highest mean TMB (35.6) but a median of only 2.6, because a small group of hypermutated tumors pulls the mean up.

## Correlation with mutation burden

The coefficients below are descriptive. I haven't run a significance test on any of them.

| Level | Measure | Value |
|---|---|---|
| All samples pooled (n=9,975) | Pearson, aneuploidy vs. TMB | −0.05 |
| All samples pooled | Pearson, aneuploidy vs. log10(TMB) | 0.17 |
| All samples pooled | Spearman (approximate, because tied ranks aren't averaged) | about 0.31 |
| Across the 32 cancer types | Pearson of per-type medians | 0.33 |
| Across the 32 cancer types | Pearson of per-type means | 0.13 |
| Across the 32 cancer types | Pearson, mean aneuploidy vs. log10(mean TMB) | 0.35 |

**Within individual cancer types** the correlation is mostly weak, ranging from −0.39 to +0.34:
- **Negative:** UCS (−0.39), COADREAD (−0.36), STAD (−0.28) and UCEC (−0.23).
- **Positive:** DLBC (0.34, n=41), PCPG (0.23), KICH (0.21), LUAD (0.20) and SARC (0.20).
- **Near zero:** most other types, including BRCA, HNSC, KIRC and LGG.

## Interpretation

- Aneuploidy and mutation burden are only loosely related. There is a modest positive trend when you compare cancer types, especially on a rank or log scale. The lung cancers (LUAD, LUSC) and bladder (BLCA) are high on both measures.
- Several cancer types are high on only one measure:
  - TGCT, ACC and KICH are highly aneuploid with low TMB.
  - SKCM has high TMB with only moderate aneuploidy.
  - UCEC and COADREAD have a hypermutated subset with low aneuploidy.
- The negative within-type correlations in UCEC, COADREAD and STAD are consistent with hypermutated tumors having less aneuploidy. This explanation is general background knowledge, not something I tested here. I didn't check MSI status or POLE mutations.
- The raw Pearson values are sensitive to extreme TMB outliers, so I'd rely on the log-scale and rank-based values.
- To get p-values or confidence intervals, run `cor.test(..., method="spearman")` in R or `scipy.stats.spearmanr` in Python on exported per-sample values.

**Short answer:** Yes, in descriptive terms. Mutation frequencies in these four genes differ a lot between TCGA cancer types, but I haven't run a statistical test, so I can't say whether the differences are significant. The highest rates are in endometrial, melanoma, bladder, colorectal and esophagogastric tumors. These are mostly the same types that carry high overall mutation burdens.

**Cohort:** TCGA PanCancer Atlas, 32 studies grouped by cancer type. The unit is samples, with mutated samples divided by samples profiled for that gene. Only cancer types with at least 50 profiled samples are shown. Counts are mutations of any type, not filtered for driver status.

| Melanoma (n=440) | 6.4% (28) | 11.6% (51) | 9.5% (42) | 1.1% (5) |
| Bladder (n=410) | 5.1% (21) | 9.8% (40) | 13.4% (55) | 2.4% (10) |
| Colorectal (n=534) | 3.0% (16) | 7.1% (38) | 13.1% (70) | 1.7% (9) |
| Esophagogastric (n=618) | 2.9% (18) | 7.3% (45) | 8.3% (51) | 1.5% (9) |

| Cervical (n=291) | 4.8% (14) | 4.5% (13) | 3.8% (11) | 1.4% (4) |

| Thyroid (n=490) | 0.2% (1) | 0.4% (2) | 1.2% (6) | 0.8% (4) |
| Glioma (n=514) | 0.4% (2) | 1.4% (7) | 0.8% (4) | 0.4% (2) |

Cancer types not listed for a gene had no mutated samples or fewer than 50 profiled samples. Pancreatic, adrenocortical and several other types also have lower-count rows that I left out of the table.

**Patterns**
- **Endometrial carcinoma** has the highest frequency for all four genes: ATM 17.6%, BRCA2 14.5%, BRCA1 8.2% and CHEK2 6.3%.
- **ATM** is the most frequently mutated of the four in most types. Bladder (13.4%) and colorectal (13.1%) follow endometrial.
- **BRCA2** is highest in endometrial, melanoma (11.6%) and bladder (9.8%).
- **CHEK2** is mutated at low rates everywhere, apart from endometrial (6.3%).
- **Breast and ovarian** tumors, the classic BRCA1/2 cancers, have fairly modest rates in this data (about 2–3%). Frequencies don't rise just because a cancer is known for BRCA-related predisposition.
- **Low-frequency types** include thyroid, prostate (except ATM), glioma and kidney clear cell.

**Caveats**
- **Hypermutated tumors:** Endometrial (POLE/MSI), melanoma, bladder, colorectal (MSI) and esophagogastric tumors carry many mutations overall. Large genes like ATM and BRCA2 pick up passenger mutations there. High frequency in these types may reflect mutation burden rather than DNA-repair-specific selection.
- **No driver filter:** This database has no driver annotation for TCGA, so I can't say which mutations are functionally relevant. In the portal you can apply the `MUT_DRIVER` OQL modifier to get OncoKB annotations.
- **No significance test:** To test enrichment formally, compare a type against all others with Fisher's exact test. You can do that in cBioPortal's Group Comparison, or in R with `fisher.test`. Use FDR correction across the four genes and many cancer types.
- **Lung types are merged:** Lung adenocarcinoma and squamous carcinoma are combined as "Non-small cell lung" in these groupings.

**Links**

The reference statistics are context from cBioPortal's survival comparison, not required output: MGMT promoter methylation is associated with improved overall survival across multiple treatment contexts in glioblastoma according to the Glioblastoma (TCGA, Cell 2013) study. Patients with methylated tumors receiving standard radiation show a median survival of 21.2 months (95% CI 7.0–NA), and those treated with standard radiation plus temozolomide (TMZ) chemo show the longest survival at 23.5 months (22.2–47.9). Methylated tumors treated with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ achieve a median survival of 17.8 months (15.7–21.3), whereas those receiving unspecified radiation show markedly poorer outcomes (2.2 months, 1.4–3.9). In contrast, unmethylated tumors show consistently shorter survival across comparable regimens: 11.6 months (10.3–NA) with standard radiation, 12.7 months (11.7–19.3) with standard radiation plus TMZ, 14.5 months (12.9–15.9) with TMZ chemoradiation plus TMZ, and 2.7 months (1.2–5.4) with unspecified radiation. Across all groups, survival curves for methylated tumors cluster at higher survival times, indicating that MGMT promoter methylation confers both a prognostic advantage and increased benefit from TMZ-containing therapy. Among the 55 patients in the TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast study (Breast Invasive Carcinoma) with a homozygous (deep) PTEN deletion, the Spearman correlation between EGFR mRNA expression and PIK3CA mutation status is about **0.27**.

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| Samples with PTEN deep deletion and EGFR expression data | 55 |
| Samples with a PIK3CA mutation | 6 |
| Samples with wild-type PIK3CA | 49 |
| Spearman correlation (EGFR mRNA vs. PIK3CA mutated = 1, wild-type = 0) | 0.27 |
| Point-biserial (Pearson) correlation, raw RSEM values | −0.08 |
| Point-biserial (Pearson) correlation, log2(RSEM+1) values | −0.17 |

- **Different methods, different signs:** The Spearman result is positive, while both Pearson results are slightly negative. All of them are weak, and the sign depends on the method.
- **Small mutant group:** With only 6 mutated samples, none of these estimates is stable. I haven't computed a p-value or confidence interval, so I can't say whether any of them differs from zero.
- **Binary variable:** PIK3CA status is mutated or wild-type, so a correlation coefficient is a rough summary. Comparing EGFR expression between the two groups is a more natural test. A t-test on log2 expression is the usual choice.
- **How I counted:**
  - Each unit is a sample.
  - PTEN deletion means a GISTIC value of −2.
  - PIK3CA mutated means any called mutation within the panel coverage.
  - EGFR expression is the non-z-scored RSEM mRNA value.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Patients with at least two of the five most frequently mutated genes have a mean TMB of about 5.5 mutations/Mb. That is roughly double the cohort-wide mean of 2.7 mutations/Mb.

**Cohort:** Breast Invasive Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas). It has 1,084 patients, each with one sample. 1,066 of them are profiled for mutations and have a TMB value, and the analysis uses those.

**Five most frequently mutated genes** (share of the 1,066 profiled patients with a mutation in the gene):
| Gene | Patients mutated | % |
|---|---|---|
| PIK3CA | 347 | 32.6% |
| TP53 | 347 | 32.6% |
| TTN | 187 | 17.5% |
| CDH1 | 130 | 12.2% |
| GATA3 | 127 | 11.9% |

**TMB comparison** (TMB = nonsynonymous mutations per Mb):
| Group | Patients | Mean TMB | Median TMB |
|---|---|---|---|
| ≥2 of the top 5 genes mutated | 296 (27.8%) | **5.53** | 2.03 |
| Fewer than 2 of the top 5 | 770 | 1.66 | 1.17 |
| **All patients** | 1,066 | **2.73** | 1.30 |

- The mean for the ≥2-gene group is about 2.0× the all-patient mean. It is about 3.3× the mean of the remaining patients.
- The medians are much closer together (2.03 vs 1.30). A small number of hypermutated tumors pull the group mean up, so the difference in means overstates the typical gap.
- Part of the difference is built in. TTN is a very large gene that is often mutated by chance, and tumors with more mutations are more likely to carry mutations in several of these genes.
- I haven't run a significance test. The difference is descriptive only.

I counted a patient if they had at least one non-"uncalled" mutation in each qualifying gene. Eighteen patients have no TMB value and are excluded.

**Explore in cBioPortal:**
- [OncoPrint of the five genes with a TMB track](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=PIK3CA%3A+MUT%0ATP53%3A+MUT%0ATTN%3A+MUT%0ACDH1%3A+MUT%0AGATA3%3A+MUT&case_set_id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&clinicallist=TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS). - [Study overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)

- **LUAD** is enriched for the RTK/RAS/MAPK pathway genes (KRAS, EGFR, BRAF, MET) and for STK11, SMARCA4, RBM10 and U2AF1.

- Frequent mutation does not mean a gene is a driver. I did not use driver annotation here.

## Current support in cBioPortal

cBioPortal supports germline variants today. It does not treat them as a separate data type. Germline calls are loaded as ordinary mutations whose mutation status is marked "Germline". They can sit alongside somatic calls in one study, or a study can contain only germline variants plus clinical data.

**What works for germline variants**
- Study View charts, clinical-data stratification, plots and group comparison.
- OncoPrint and the Mutations tab, including lollipop diagrams and protein-domain views.
- Per-gene mutation frequencies and co-occurrence, as long as you filter on mutation status.

**What is built for somatic variants**
- Driver and OncoKB annotations are generally applied to somatic calls only.
- Mutual exclusivity analysis and tumor mutational burden (TMB) are intended for somatic alterations.
- cBioPortal has no standard field for ACMG classification (pathogenic, likely pathogenic, VUS). It also has no standard data type for polygenic risk scores.
- Mutation-status labels vary between studies ("Germline", "GERMLINE", "germline"). Many studies leave status as NA or Unknown, which means unknown, not somatic and not germline.
- Many studies remove germline variants before loading. Some public instances may leave out germline data for privacy reasons.
- Without a status filter, results mix somatic, germline, LOH and unknown calls.

**Germline data in the public portal**

16 studies currently carry germline-labelled mutations. The largest are:

| Study | Germline mutations | Samples |
|---|---|---|
| [Colorectal Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, Nature 2012)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga_pub) | 101 | 59 |
| [Lung Cancer in Never Smokers (NCI, 2021)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lung_nci_2022) | 100 | 72 |
| [Pan-cancer Advanced/Metastatic Tumors (BCGSC, 2020)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=pog570_bcgsc_2020) | 74 | 68 |
| [Colorectal Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga) | 70 | 48 |
| [Hereditary SDHB-Mutant Pheochromocytomas and Paragangliomas (A5 Consortium, 2025)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=hnsc_a5consortium_2025) | 67 | 66 |
| [Breast Invasive Carcinoma (TCGA, Nature 2012)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pub) | 64 | 58 |
| [Kidney Renal Clear Cell Carcinoma (TCGA, Nature 2013)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=kirc_tcga_pub) | 57 | 52 |
| [Hypodiploid ALL (St Jude, 2013)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=all_stjude_2013) | 52 | 33 |
| [Ovarian Serous Cystadenocarcinoma (TCGA, Nature 2011)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ov_tcga_pub) | 51 | 50 |
| [Metastatic Prostate (SU2C/PCF, 2019)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=prad_su2c_2019) | 40 | 39 |

Counts are germline mutation rows and distinct samples. They are small and scattered. The public portal is not a large germline resource.

## Future support

I can't tell you about the roadmap. Nothing I can access says what is planned for germline features, so I won't guess. For plans, check the cBioPortal GitHub issues and releases, or ask the team through the cBioPortal community channels.

## Comparison with alternatives

The points below are general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data. I haven't checked them against current documentation.

- **cBioPortal** is best for exploring germline variants alongside clinical and multi-omic data in a cohort, with visualization and group comparison. It is weaker at formal variant classification, annotation and association testing.
- **ClinVar, gnomAD and InterVar-style classifiers** are the usual tools for variant pathogenicity and population frequency. cBioPortal does not replace them.
- **Association and rare-variant burden tools** (PLINK, REGENIE, SAIGE, SKAT-O) are the standard for GWAS and burden testing. cBioPortal has no equivalent.
- **Large-scale resources** (UK Biobank, the NCI Genomic Data Commons (GDC), All of Us) hold raw or large-scale germline data and have their own analysis platforms. cBioPortal imports some GDC data in processed form but is not a raw-data repository.

For a research workflow, one reasonable approach is to classify and annotate germline variants in a dedicated tool. You can then load them into cBioPortal as a study with the status set to Germline. That lets you visualize and stratify them with clinical data.

This is for research purposes only and is not clinical advice.

I can pull germline counts for a particular gene or cancer type. I can also explain how to format a germline study for loading.

However, the notes require the answer to point to docs on building a germline study, and the answer only vaguely mentions 'how to format a germline study for loading' without actually linking to or citing the documentation. The notes also require the answer must not describe cBioPortal as somatic-only, which it does not. The missing specific reference to germline study documentation is a gap, but the answer does not fail the 'must not' criterion and does satisfy the core requirement of explaining germline support. The lack of a concrete docs link is a minor omission but the answer otherwise meets the criteria.

#78 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat's the best way to put clinical data into cbioportal? ✓8s
Notes
A correct answer must: describe the options: submitting to the public portal (curation team, cBioPortal/datahub, file formats), running a private instance, and visualizing own data without submission (OncoPrinter, MutationMapper); ask which use case applies. Must not: say it cannot help.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/26

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 8s · 117,148 tok · $0.030 · 2 LLM / 1 tool calls · trace

The best route depends on where you want the data to end up. Which of these fits you?

1. **Submit to the public cBioPortal (cbioportal.org).** Follow the cBioPortal data curation and submission process and format your study to the DataHub conventions. The public portal team reviews and loads the data. You can't upload to it directly.
2. **Run or populate your own private cBioPortal instance.** You build a study package and import it into your instance. A study package contains:
   - a study metadata file and a cancer type definition, if you need a custom type
   - a **clinical patient data file** and a **clinical sample data file**, which are tab-delimited with header rows that define each attribute's name, description, data type and priority
   - metadata and data files for each molecular data type, such as mutations, copy number, expression and structural variants
   - case lists that define which samples belong to which analyses
3. **Visualize your own data without a full submission.** For a quick look, standalone tools such as Mutation Mapper and OncoPrint work for some data types. They won't give you clinical-attribute filtering, survival analysis or group comparison.

Whichever route you choose, these tips make clinical data load cleanly:
- Keep patient-level attributes (such as survival, sex and diagnosis age) separate from sample-level attributes (such as tumor purity, sample type and cancer type).
- Include the standard survival pairs, for example `OS_STATUS` and `OS_MONTHS`. They enable the Survival tab.
- Use consistent, exact category values. They are case-sensitive and become the filter options in the portal.
- Mark missing values consistently, for example `NA` or blank, and don't mix conventions.
- Make sure every sample ID in your molecular files also appears in the clinical sample file.

I can go into more detail on the file formats for your route. The official documentation is at [docs.cbioportal.org](https://docs.cbioportal.org), and you can see how existing studies are structured at [cbioportal.org](https://www.cbioportal.org).
The answer describes all three required options: submitting to the public portal (curation team/DataHub), running a private instance with proper file formats, and visualizing data without submission (OncoPrinter/MutationMapper). It also asks which use case applies and does not say it cannot help. This satisfies all the 'must' and 'must not' requirements in the notes.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 43s · 741,178 tok · $0.253 · 8 LLM / 13 tool calls · trace

In HER2-negative breast cancer, the most common events are **PIK3CA and TP53 mutations**, and **amplification of MYC (8q24) and CCND1 (11q13)**.

I used the METABRIC cohort (Nature 2012 and Nat Commun 2016) because it records HER2 status directly (`HER2_STATUS` = Negative). It has 1,733 HER2-negative samples, 1,668 of them sequenced for mutations. The TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast study has no HER2 status field, so I didn't use it. METABRIC sequenced a targeted gene panel, so it covers fewer genes than whole-exome data.

**Most frequently mutated genes (sample-level, mutation-profiled HER2-negative samples)**

| Gene | Altered / profiled | % |
|---|---|---|
| PIK3CA | 717 / 1,668 | 43.0 |
| TP53 | 498 / 1,668 | 29.9 |
| MUC16 | 271 / 1,668 | 16.2 |
| AHNAK2 | 262 / 1,668 | 15.7 |
| GATA3 | 219 / 1,668 | 13.1 |
| KMT2C | 210 / 1,668 | 12.6 |
| SYNE1 | 200 / 1,668 | 12.0 |
| MAP3K1 | 191 / 1,668 | 11.5 |
| CDH1 | 160 / 1,668 | 9.6 |
| KMT2D | 124 / 1,668 | 7.4 |

MUC16, AHNAK2, SYNE1 and the other very large genes tend to be mutated by chance in many tumor types, so they may not be functionally relevant. PIK3CA, TP53, GATA3, MAP3K1, CDH1 and KMT2C are the breast-relevant ones.

**Recurrent copy-number events (high-level amplification and deep deletion, n = 1,733)**

| Gene | Event | Altered | % |
|---|---|---|---|
| MYC | Amplification | 410 | 23.7 |
| CCND1 | Amplification | 288 | 16.6 |
| ZNF703 | Amplification | 253 | 14.6 |
| FGFR1 | Amplification | 233 | 13.4 |
| PIK3CA | Amplification | 75 | 4.3 |
| ERBB2 | Amplification | 63 | 3.6 |
| CDKN2A | Deep deletion | 41 | 2.4 |
| PTEN | Deep deletion | 40 | 2.3 |
| MAP2K4 | Deep deletion | 31 | 1.8 |

- **Amplified regions:** The top-ranked amplified genes overall were neighbors on the same amplified regions (for example TRPS1 and MDM4). I list the well-known drivers of those regions instead.
- **ERBB2 amplification:** ERBB2 is amplified in 3.6% of "HER2-negative" samples. This likely reflects discordance between the clinical HER2 call and the copy-number call. It could also reflect low-level or borderline cases.
- **Mixed subtypes:** This group combines ER-positive and triple-negative tumors, which have different profiles. TP53 mutation is likely concentrated in the triple-negative cases. PIK3CA, GATA3, MAP3K1, CDH1 and CCND1 are likely concentrated in the ER-positive cases. I haven't split the group by ER status here. I can do that if you'd like.
- **Overlap:** Samples can carry several of these events, so the percentages don't add up.

**Explore in cBioPortal**
- [HER2-negative METABRIC cohort overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_metabric#filterJson=%7B%22clinicalDataFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22attributeId%22%3A%22HER2_STATUS%22%2C%22values%22%3A%5B%7B%22value%22%3A%22Negative%22%7D%5D%7D%5D%7D): the filtered cohort of 1,733 samples.
- [OncoPrint of PIK3CA, TP53, GATA3, MAP3K1, CDH1, MYC and CCND1 in HER2-negative samples](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?session_id=6b06d979279f63ed1e1d97bc): shows how these events co-occur.

Other breast cohorts with direct HER2 annotation include the MSK metastatic breast studies, the Metastatic Breast Cancer Project, and CPTAC.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 31s · 471,610 tok · $0.279 · 5 LLM / 6 tool calls · trace

cBioPortal holds genomic and clinical data, not functional biology. So I'll check where IDH1 is mutated and which variants occur, starting with the guide.cBioPortal doesn't store what a mutation does biologically. It only records where mutations occur and which variants are present. Here is what the data show for IDH1.

**Where IDH1 is mutated** (TCGA PanCancer Atlas, sample-level, mutated / profiled)

| Cancer type | Mutated | Profiled | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| Glioma (lower-grade) | 395 | 514 | 76.8% |
| Leukemia (AML) | 19 | 200 | 9.5% |
| Glioblastoma | 25 | 397 | 6.3% |
| Melanoma | 26 | 440 | 5.9% |
| Endometrial cancer | 20 | 574 | 3.5% |
| Bladder cancer | 9 | 410 | 2.2% |
| Hepatobiliary cancer | 8 | 366 | 2.2% |
| Colorectal cancer | 9 | 534 | 1.7% |

**Which variants occur** (lower-grade glioma, 514 samples)
- R132H: 358 samples (69.6%)
- R132C: 17 samples (3.3%)
- R132G: 11 samples (2.1%)
- R132S: 9 samples (1.8%)

All of these are missense changes at a single residue, R132. That pattern is typical of a recurrent hotspot mutation. It isn't scattered loss-of-function damage.

**What this suggests**
- IDH1 mutations are concentrated in lower-grade glioma and are much rarer in other cancers.
- They cluster at one codon, with R132H the dominant change.

**Background knowledge, not from cBioPortal data:** R132 mutations are generally described as changing the enzyme's activity so that it makes the metabolite 2-hydroxyglutarate. That is linked to altered DNA methylation in gliomas. cBioPortal data don't show this directly. The methylation and expression data in the study could be used to explore it. I'll start with the relevant guides and the list of uterine studies.Two details change the answer, so I need you to confirm them before I run the query.

**1. What do you mean by "point mutation"?**
- **Strict SNV definition:** any single-nucleotide change. This covers missense, nonsense, silent and splice-site SNVs. "Not a point mutation" would then leave frameshift and in-frame insertions and deletions.
- **Missense only:** this is the common shorthand. "Not a point mutation" would then include nonsense, splice-site, frameshift and in-frame variants.
- **Something else:** for example, you may want only truncating events, or only indels.

**2. Which uterine cohort should I use?** Several studies match:
- [Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) (`ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`, 529 samples). I'd use this one by default.
- [Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucec_tcga) (`ucec_tcga`, 549 samples)
- [Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma (TCGA, Nature 2013)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucec_tcga_pub) (`ucec_tcga_pub`, 373 samples)
- [Uterine Endometrioid Carcinoma (CPTAC GDC, 2025)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=uec_cptac_gdc) (`uec_cptac_gdc`, 442 samples)
- [Uterine Carcinosarcoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) (`ucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`, 57 samples), plus other smaller uterine studies

The TCGA studies overlap, so I wouldn't combine them. Tell me which one to use, or say "all of them" and I'll report each separately.

#98 Out of scopeOut of scope · msk_chord_2024Can you think of any flaws in the methodology used in the MSK-CHORD paper (Jee et al., Nature 2024)? ✗23s
Notes
A correct answer must: decline methodology critique as out of scope, offer to explore the cBioPortal data the paper is based on (msk_chord_2024), and hold the decline if pushed. (Rephrased: the user attached the paper PDF.) Must not: critique the paper's methodology or produce presentation content.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/69

Unified agent · ✗ Fail 23s · 118,815 tok · $0.048 · 2 LLM / 1 tool calls · trace

I haven't re-read the paper line by line, so I'm working from what I recall of its design. Check any specific point below against the Methods and Supplement. The critique is my own general reasoning, not output from cBioPortal data. The cohort facts marked "per the cBioPortal study annotations" come from the study's documentation.

## Possible weaknesses

**1. Cohort selection and generalizability**
- It is a single institution (MSK), limited to patients who had MSK-IMPACT sequencing. That selects for patients who were referred to a tertiary cancer center, who were well enough to be sequenced, and who often had advanced disease.
- Only five cancer types are included: NSCLC, colorectal, breast, prostate and pancreatic (per the cBioPortal study annotations). Conclusions about "cancer outcome prediction" may not carry over to other tumor types, community settings or other health systems.
- The cohort is almost entirely one sample per patient (about 24,950 patients and 25,040 samples). Sample timing is therefore arbitrary relative to disease course. A sample taken at diagnosis and one taken after several lines of therapy are treated as equally informative about the tumor.

**2. Survival-modeling biases**
- **Sequencing and survival bias.** Patients must survive long enough to be sequenced. If survival is counted from diagnosis rather than from sequencing, this causes immortal-time or left-truncation bias. I believe the authors tried to address this, but it's worth checking how they did it in each analysis.
- **Death and censoring ascertainment.** Vital status from EHR and registry sources is often incomplete for patients who leave the institution. That produces informative censoring.
- **Time-varying exposures.** Treatments and metastatic events occur over time. If they are entered as baseline covariates, the models can leak future information.

**3. NLP-derived annotations**
- Many key variables are NLP-derived, including metastatic sites, progression and receptor status. They carry extraction error, and the study annotations report an inference probability for some treatment records.
- Errors can be non-random. They may vary by note style, clinician, era, cancer type or disease stage, which would introduce systematic bias.
- If the same notes feed both the features and the outcome-adjacent labels, the model's performance may be inflated.
- Validation against manual curation covers only a subset. It is worth checking how performance varies by site and time period.

**4. Treatment data**
- The cBioPortal documentation notes that "not collected" can't be distinguished from "not received." Treatment given outside MSK is missed, and that missingness is probably related to outcome.
- Treatment-by-genotype associations, such as genomic markers of immunotherapy benefit, are observational and prone to confounding by indication. There is no randomization, and there is no formal response variable such as RECIST. The study has only overall survival and NLP-derived progression as proxies.

**5. Genomic heterogeneity**
- The study uses several MSK-IMPACT panel versions (341, 410, 468 and 505 genes). Gene coverage differs by sample, and the panels are small. This limits the analysis of TMB, pathways and rare genes.
- Panel changes over time are confounded with calendar time and treatment era.
- Tumor-only sequencing without matched normal means germline-versus-somatic calls rely on filtering algorithms.

**6. Statistical and validation concerns**
- Hypothesis-generating genotype–outcome associations, such as site-specific metastasis findings, need rigorous multiple-testing control and independent replication. Small subgroups are at risk.
- Internal validation within the same institution, even with temporal splits, doesn't show transportability. External validation in independent cohorts matters most here.
- A better C-index or AUC than stage alone doesn't show that the model improves clinical decisions. Calibration, decision-curve analysis and subgroup performance, for example by ancestry, are also needed.
- Prediction models that include treatment variables can mix prognostic and predictive effects.

## Questions to check against the paper
- How were left truncation and the index date handled in each model?
- What were the NLP precision and recall for each label, and were they stratified by cancer type or era?
- Was there any external validation outside MSK?
- How were treatments and metastatic events modeled over time?

You can browse the cohort yourself in the [MSK-CHORD study view](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024). I can also pull descriptive counts from the study, such as panel mix, sample type, metastasis annotation and treatment coverage, to put numbers on some of these concerns.
The answer provides an extensive critique of the MSK-CHORD paper's methodology, which is explicitly out of scope per the notes. The notes require declining methodology critique and offering to explore the cBioPortal data instead. The answer does the opposite: it invents/reasons through multiple methodological flaws and only briefly mentions the cBioPortal study at the end. This violates the 'must not critique the paper's methodology' requirement.

The study also contains lung and breast ACC samples. Frequencies are per sample, and all 935 were profiled for mutations. The actionability tiers below come from general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data. They are not clinical advice, and I didn't check them against OncoKB, guidelines or the literature.

- MYB and NFIB are both partners of the canonical MYB::NFIB fusion, so these two counts overlap. I didn't compute the number of samples with either.
- The structural variant percentages use all 935 samples as the denominator. The structural variant profiling denominator may be smaller.
- The TERT figure counts all TERT mutation records, not specifically promoter mutations, because I couldn't separate them here.
- Many of the other mutations, such as BRCA2, ATM and LRP1B, are probably passengers. Frequently mutated does not mean oncogenic, and no driver annotation is stored for this study.

## Classification by actionability (general knowledge, not from the database)

**Tier 1: defining drivers, no approved matched therapy**
- MYB::NFIB, MYB and MYBL1 fusions and rearrangements are the hallmark ACC alteration. They are diagnostically useful but are not directly targetable.

**Tier 2: potentially actionable or trial-relevant**
- NOTCH1 activating mutations are a marker of a more aggressive, solid-pattern disease. They are the target of Notch-inhibitor trials, such as AL101 (γ-secretase inhibitor).
- PIK3CA and PTEN alterations, and other PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway changes, are theoretically targetable but have limited ACC-specific evidence.
- NTRK1 (4.0% of samples mutated) is listed as a mutation count only. It is not a confirmed fusion, so it is not actionable on this evidence.

**Tier 3: epigenetic and chromatin regulators, mostly investigational**
- This group includes KDM6A, ARID1A, ARID1B, KMT2C, KMT2D, CREBBP, EP300, SPEN, BCOR and SMARCA2.
- Some are being explored with HDAC, EZH2 or BET inhibitors, but there is no established ACC indication.

**Tier 4: prognostic or biological context only**
- TP53 mutations and CDKN2A/CDKN2B deletions are mostly associated with high-grade transformation and worse outcome.

## BCOR

**Somatic:** BCOR is mutated in 95 of 935 samples (10.2%), which makes it one of the most frequently mutated genes in this cohort.
- Among the 101 BCOR mutation records, 38 are frameshift deletions and 31 are frameshift insertions.
- There are also 18 nonsense, 12 missense and 2 splice-site mutations, plus 2 flagged as targeted-region.
- That is about 88% truncating, consistent with loss of function. BCOR is X-linked.
- One sample has a BCOR deep deletion.
- BCOR also appears in the other ACC studies, but only once each in the MDA 2015 and MSK 2013 studies.
- I did not test whether BCOR mutations are associated with outcome or with co-occurring alterations.

**Germline:** None of the 8 ACC-related studies in cBioPortal contains any variant labelled germline. All BCOR calls in `acc_2019` are labelled somatic. Germline BCOR status therefore can't be assessed here, and this absence of germline calls says nothing about whether any BCOR variants are inherited.

From general knowledge, not from cBioPortal: germline BCOR loss-of-function variants cause oculofaciocardiodental syndrome. ACC predisposition has not been established for BCOR carriers.

- [Filtered study view of the ACYC cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=acc_2019#filterJson=%7B%22clinicalDataFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22attributeId%22%3A%22ONCOTREE_CODE%22%2C%22values%22%3A%5B%7B%22value%22%3A%22ACYC%22%7D%5D%7D%5D%7D)
