I'll check the msk_chord study ID and how its timeline data is organized, so the code matches the real data.This is Python for both ways of getting the MSK-CHORD (`msk_chord_2024`) timeline data. I haven't run either one, and I wrote the file names and the study-level API endpoint from memory.

- **Option A** reads the `data_timeline_*.txt` files from a local download of the study.
- **Option B** queries the public cBioPortal REST API, so you don't need to download anything.

The study has these timeline event types: `Treatment`, `Diagnosis`, `Lab_Test`, `Pathology`, `Surgery`, `Sequencing` and `Sample acquisition`. All dates are days from diagnosis.

## Option A: local timeline files

Download the study from the [cBioPortal datasets page](https://www.cbioportal.org/datasets) or the DataHub repo. Then point `STUDY_DIR` at the folder.

```python
from pathlib import Path
import pandas as pd

STUDY_DIR = Path("msk_chord_2024")   # folder containing data_timeline_*.txt


def load_timelines(study_dir: Path = STUDY_DIR) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Read every data_timeline_*.txt file into one long DataFrame."""
    frames = []
    for f in sorted(study_dir.glob("data_timeline*.txt")):
        df = pd.read_csv(f, sep="\t", dtype=str, low_memory=False)
        df["SOURCE_FILE"] = f.name
        frames.append(df)
    if not frames:
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"No data_timeline*.txt files in {study_dir}")
    tl = pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True)
    for col in ("START_DATE", "STOP_DATE"):
        if col in tl:
            tl[col] = pd.to_numeric(tl[col], errors="coerce")
    return tl


tl = load_timelines()

# --- Overview -------------------------------------------------------------
print(tl["EVENT_TYPE"].value_counts())
print(tl.groupby("SOURCE_FILE")["PATIENT_ID"].nunique())

# --- Treatment events: agent + category ------------------------------------
tx = tl[tl["EVENT_TYPE"].str.upper() == "TREATMENT"]
print(tx.groupby("AGENT")["PATIENT_ID"].nunique()
        .sort_values(ascending=False).head(20))

# Chemo only
chemo = tx[tx["SUBTYPE"] == "Chemo"]

# --- One patient's timeline ------------------------------------------------
def patient_timeline(patient_id: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    cols = [c for c in ("EVENT_TYPE", "START_DATE", "STOP_DATE", "SUBTYPE", "AGENT")
            if c in tl.columns]
    return (tl[tl["PATIENT_ID"] == patient_id]
            .sort_values("START_DATE")[cols])

# --- Treatments that started before sequencing ----------------------------
seq = (tl[tl["EVENT_TYPE"] == "Sequencing"]
       .groupby("PATIENT_ID")["START_DATE"].min().rename("SEQ_DATE"))
tx_seq = tx.join(seq, on="PATIENT_ID")
pre_seq = tx_seq[tx_seq["START_DATE"] < tx_seq["SEQ_DATE"]]
print(pre_seq.groupby("AGENT")["PATIENT_ID"].nunique()
             .sort_values(ascending=False).head(20))
```

## Option B: public REST API

```python
import requests
import pandas as pd

BASE = "https://www.cbioportal.org/api"
STUDY_ID = "msk_chord_2024"
HEADERS = {"Accept": "application/json"}
# For a private portal, add: HEADERS["Authorization"] = "Bearer "


def events_to_df(events: list[dict]) -> pd.DataFrame:
    rows = []
    for e in events:
        row = {
            "patientId": e.get("patientId"),
            "eventType": e.get("eventType"),
            "start": e.get("startNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis"),
            "stop": e.get("endNumberOfDaysSinceDiagnosis"),
        }
        # attributes come back as [{"key": "AGENT", "value": "..."}, ...]
        row.update({a["key"]: a["value"] for a in e.get("attributes", [])})
        rows.append(row)
    return pd.DataFrame(rows)


def get_patient_events(patient_id: str) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """All timeline events for a single patient."""
    url = f"{BASE}/studies/{STUDY_ID}/patients/{patient_id}/clinical-events"
    r = requests.get(url, headers=HEADERS, params={"projection": "DETAILED"}, timeout=60)
    r.raise_for_status()
    return events_to_df(r.json())


def get_study_events(page_size: int = 10000, max_pages: int | None = None) -> pd.DataFrame:
    """Study-wide events, paged. MSK-CHORD has millions of events, so
    limit pages while testing."""
    url = f"{BASE}/studies/{STUDY_ID}/clinical-events"
    frames, page = [], 0
    while max_pages is None or page < max_pages:
        r = requests.get(url, headers=HEADERS, timeout=120,
                         params={"projection": "DETAILED",
                                 "pageSize": page_size, "pageNumber": page})
        r.raise_for_status()
        batch = r.json()
        if not batch:
            break
        frames.append(events_to_df(batch))
        page += 1
    return pd.concat(frames, ignore_index=True) if frames else pd.DataFrame()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Sample the first 5 pages to check the structure
    df = get_study_events(page_size=5000, max_pages=5)
    print(df["eventType"].value_counts())

    tx = df[df["eventType"].str.upper() == "TREATMENT"]
    print(tx.groupby("AGENT")["patientId"].nunique()
            .sort_values(ascending=False).head(10))
```

## Notes

- **Treatment fields:** treatment events carry `AGENT`, `SUBTYPE`, `RX_INVESTIGATIVE`, `FLAG_OROTOPICAL`, and (for NLP-derived prior medications) `PRIOR_MED_TO_MSK` and `INFERRED_TX_PROB`. Filter on these if you want to exclude investigational or prior-to-MSK drugs.
- **Event-type casing:** I match `Treatment` case-insensitively because some studies use `TREATMENT`.
- **Counts:** report patient counts rather than percentages. The data can't separate "never received" from "not recorded".
- **Size:** the study has well over a million timeline events, mostly `Diagnosis` rows. In the API version, pass `max_pages` or filter by patient first. In the file version, you can use `usecols=` or read only the treatment file to save memory.
- **Check the files first:** run `print(sorted(STUDY_DIR.glob("data_timeline*")))` to confirm your download's file names. If the study-level endpoint isn't available on your portal version, loop `get_patient_events` over patient IDs.

I can also adapt this to a specific question, such as treatment sequences or time from sequencing to first immunotherapy. I can also build a cBioPortal link for a cohort you want to look at.
The answer provides Python code using the public cBioPortal REST API (Option B uses `/api/studies/{studyId}/clinical-events`) and also offers a local file download option. It does not use ClickHouse credentials. The code is well-structured and correctly targets the public API endpoint. This satisfies the 'must' requirement of writing code against the public REST API and the 'must not' requirement of avoiding ClickHouse credentials.

Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/98

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 40s · 559,894 tok · $0.169 · 8 LLM / 13 tool calls · trace

I couldn't confirm that any study has a file named `data_clinical_outcomes.txt`. My database holds the loaded data, not the original upload files or their names, so I can't see file names.

I also don't think cBioPortal has a standard file by that name. The documented clinical formats are:

- `data_clinical_patient.txt` with `meta_clinical_patient.txt`
- `data_clinical_sample.txt` with `meta_clinical_sample.txt`
- `data_timeline_.txt` with `meta_timeline_.txt`

Outcome data is normally loaded in one of two ways:

1. **Patient file:** outcome columns such as `OS_STATUS`, `OS_MONTHS`, `DFS_STATUS` and `PFS_STATUS` sit in the patient clinical file.
2. **Timeline file:** outcome events such as status, progression or recurrence sit in a timeline file.

If a collaborator or tutorial told you to use `data_clinical_outcomes.txt`, it may be a custom name from a private study package. You can name the file anything as long as the meta file's `data_filename` points to it. The meta file for a clinical data file uses `genetic_alteration_type: CLINICAL` and `datatype: PATIENT_ATTRIBUTES`. A timeline file uses `datatype: TIMELINE`.

## Studies to emulate
**Outcome columns in the patient file**
- [Breast Invasive Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) (`brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`): 1,084 patients have `OS_STATUS`, `DFS_STATUS` and `PFS_STATUS`. The other PanCancer Atlas TCGA studies have the same layout. This is the standard template.
- [Rectal cancer (MSK, 2022)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=rectal_msk_2022) (`rectal_msk_2022`): curated outcome and response attributes, including `RECURRENCE`, `LOCAL_RECURRENCE`, `DISTAL_RECURRENCE`, `LARC_CRT_OUTCOME_CASES_DFS`, `RESPONSE_PCR_OR_CCR` and `USE_IN_OUTCOME_ANALYSIS`.
- [Gastroesophageal cancer, TRAP (MSK, 2023)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=egc_trap_ccr_msk_2023) (`egc_trap_ccr_msk_2023`): treatment response and recurrence attributes, including `BEST_RESPONSE_CATEGORY` and `9_WEEK_RECIST_RESPONSE`.
- [Bladder, BCAN HCRN (2022)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=blca_bcan_hcrn_2022) (`blca_bcan_hcrn_2022`): best response by therapy class, such as `BEST_RESPONSE_IMMUNOTHERAPY`.

**Outcome or status as timeline events**

- [Breast Invasive Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018): the same study also has `STATUS` timeline events. They cover vital status, tumor status and staging at initial diagnosis, so it shows both approaches.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 32s · 631,109 tok · $0.205 · 7 LLM / 8 tool calls · trace

**I found no PIK3CA p.\*1069Wext\*3 in the database. The closest match is p.\*1069Wext\*4.**

**What the notation means**
- PIK3CA's normal protein ends at residue 1068. Position 1069 is the stop codon.
- The change turns the stop codon (\*) into tryptophan (W). The protein is then extended until the next stop codon. That is a stop-loss (nonstop) mutation.
- The "\*3" or "\*4" is how many residues the new tail runs before a new stop is reached. Your report says \*3. cBioPortal records \*4.
- The two may be the same event written with different counting, or two different events. I can't tell which from this data. Please check the exact HGVS/transcript notation with the lab that issued the report.

**Closest match: PIK3CA \*1069Wext\*4 (Nonstop_Mutation)**
I counted unique patients with this variant. These are raw occurrence counts only, with no frequency calculation.

| Cohort | Patients | Cancer type |
|---|---|---|
| MSK-IMPACT 50K (`msk_impact_50k_2026`) | 3 | Colon adenocarcinoma, uterine endometrioid carcinoma, high-grade serous ovarian cancer |
| MSK-CHORD 2024 | 2 | Colon adenocarcinoma (both) |
| MSK-MET 2021 | 2 | Colon adenocarcinoma, uterine endometrioid carcinoma |
| MSK pan-cancer MIMSI 2024 | 2 | Colon adenocarcinoma, uterine endometrioid carcinoma |
| CRC MSK 2026 | 2 | Colon adenocarcinoma (both) |
| TCGA GBM (several versions of the same study) | 1 | Glioblastoma, sample TCGA-32-1979-01 |
| METABRIC | 1 | Breast invasive ductal carcinoma |
| CRC DFCI 2016 | 1 | Colorectal adenocarcinoma |
| Cell lines: CCLE (IGROV1 ovary, KYSE70 oesophagus), NCI-60 | Not counted | Cell lines, not patients |

- The MSK studies overlap heavily and share the same patients. The two colon cases (P-0038429, P-0077592), the uterine case (P-0029972) and the ovarian case (P-0067686) each appear in several MSK cohorts. The TCGA GBM case also appears in several TCGA versions. So there are only about 8–9 distinct patient tumors in total. Please don't add up the table rows.
- None of these studies has driver or oncogenicity annotation in the database.

**Neighbouring variants at the same stop codon**
- \*1069Ffs\*5, a frameshift: several MSK and breast cohorts.
- \*1069Lfs\*4/\*5 and \*1069fs\*: single cases.
- Other nonstop calls at \*1064, \*1068, \*1069 and \*1069Rext\*4: a few cases.

**What cBioPortal can't tell you**
Counts of occurrences don't show whether this variant matters biologically or clinically. For that, check an external curated source such as OncoKB, ClinVar or UniProt, or the primary literature. I didn't review any of those here. I can't offer clinical interpretation.

It is exome-sequenced, so every gene has the same denominator, and the ICD-O-3 subsite codes let me assign sidedness. I'll start with that cohort.# Mutation frequency in left-sided vs right-sided colorectal cancer

I used the [TCGA PanCancer Atlas colorectal cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=coadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018). It is exome-sequenced, so every gene has the same denominator. Sidedness is not recorded directly, so I derived it from the ICD-O-3 tumor subsite code.

- **Right:** cecum, ascending colon and hepatic flexure.
- **Left:** splenic flexure, descending colon, sigmoid, rectosigmoid and rectum.
- **Excluded:** transverse colon (20 patients) and colon NOS (97), because their side is ambiguous.

The unit is patients with mutation data. That gives 170 right-sided and 254 left-sided patients.

## Selected genes

| Gene | Right-sided | Left-sided | Left − Right (points) |
|---|---|---|---|
| BRAF | 40/170 (23.5%) | 10/254 (3.9%) | −19.6 |
| PIK3CA | 62/170 (36.5%) | 45/254 (17.7%) | −18.8 |
| RNF43 | 33/170 (19.4%) | 7/254 (2.8%) | −16.6 |
| KRAS | 82/170 (48.2%) | 83/254 (32.7%) | −15.5 |
| SOX9 | 29/170 (17.1%) | 18/254 (7.1%) | −10.0 |
| ARID1A | 25/170 (14.7%) | 17/254 (6.7%) | −8.0 |
| ACVR2A | 21/170 (12.4%) | 9/254 (3.5%) | −8.9 |
| TP53 | 88/170 (51.8%) | 174/254 (68.5%) | +16.7 |
| APC | 112/170 (65.9%) | 199/254 (78.3%) | +12.4 |
| SMAD4 | 25/170 (14.7%) | 29/254 (11.4%) | −3.3 |
| FBXW7 | 30/170 (17.6%) | 37/254 (14.6%) | −3.0 |
| NRAS | 10/170 (5.9%) | 19/254 (7.5%) | +1.6 |

Beyond this list, the largest gaps across all genes were in large genes such as PCLO, KMT2D, TTN, LRP2 and ZFHX4. These were all higher on the right.

## Hypermutation confounds the comparison

Right-sided tumors in this cohort are much more often hypermutated (MSI or POLE subtypes). I counted 46 of 191 right-sided patients versus 12 of 280 left-sided. Hypermutation inflates mutation rates in large and passenger-prone genes. That likely explains much of the broad right-sided excess in genes like PCLO and TTN.

I repeated the comparison in non-hypermutated tumors only (124 right, 242 left):

| Gene | Right (non-hypermutated) | Left (non-hypermutated) |
|---|---|---|
| KRAS | 69/124 (55.6%) | 77/242 (31.8%) |
| PIK3CA | 44/124 (35.5%) | 38/242 (15.7%) |
| SOX9 | 21/124 (16.9%) | 16/242 (6.6%) |
| BRAF | 13/124 (10.5%) | 5/242 (2.1%) |
| TP53 | 75/124 (60.5%) | 167/242 (69.0%) |
| APC | 94/124 (75.8%) | 189/242 (78.1%) |
| RNF43 | 7/124 (5.6%) | 5/242 (2.1%) |

- **Hypermutation explains part of the pattern.** The RNF43 gap shrinks to about 3.5 points. The APC gap shrinks to about 2 points. The TP53 gap narrows from 16.7 to about 8.5 points.
- **Some right-sided differences persist.** KRAS, PIK3CA and SOX9 remain higher on the right in non-hypermutated tumors, and BRAF stays higher but with small counts.

## Caveats

- I haven't tested whether these differences are statistically significant. The appropriate test is Fisher's exact test with multiple-testing correction. The Group Comparison link below runs it for every gene.
- Hypermutation is defined here by TCGA's MSI and POLE molecular subtypes.
- Mutations only. Copy-number and structural events aren't included.

[View mRNA expression enrichment across LGG molecular subtypes](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/mrna?comparisonId=6b06da5dd19a2abe92916ac9)

**Cohort:** Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas). The groups use the study's `SUBTYPE` annotation, which has three subtypes:

| Subtype | Samples |
|---|---|
| LGG_IDHwt | 92 |
| LGG_IDHmut-codel | 167 |
| LGG_IDHmut-non-codel | 248 |

Samples with no subtype annotation (about 7 of the 514) are left out.

**What the page shows:** It compares mRNA expression for every gene across the three subtypes. For each gene it gives the group means and a significance test with FDR correction. Genes that are significantly higher in one subtype than in the others are the overexpressed ones.

**How to use it:**
- To see genes overexpressed in one subtype, select that group and compare it against the others. You can also pick any two groups for a pairwise test.
- Sort by q-value to find the strongest differences. Look at the log ratio as well, because a gene can be significant with only a small difference in expression.
- Expression here is z-scored within this cohort. That makes the values comparable between subtypes but not between cancer types.

**Related views:**
- [LGG PanCancer Atlas study overview](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) lets you explore each subtype's clinical features.

Subtype is patient-level; join via patient.Yes, 7p copy number differs a lot between the three TCGA lower grade glioma (LGG) molecular subtypes. Gain of 7p is far more common in IDH-wildtype tumors than in either IDH-mutant subtype. I haven't run a statistical test, so I can't give a p-value.

**Cohort:** Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas), using the arm-level copy number calls for 7p. Counts are per patient.

| Subtype | 7p gain | 7p unchanged | 7p loss | Not available | % with gain (of profiled) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| IDH-wildtype | 59 | 30 | 0 | 3 | 59/89 (66.3%) |
| IDH-mutant, non-codeleted | 29 | 214 | 0 | 5 | 29/243 (11.9%) |
| IDH-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted | 8 | 156 | 0 | 3 | 8/164 (4.9%) |

- **Direction of the difference:** 7p gain is the dominant event in IDH-wildtype tumors. It is uncommon in the non-codeleted subtype and rarer still in the codeleted subtype.
- **No 7p loss:** no patient in any subtype has a 7p loss call.
- **Missing subtype:** 7 patients have no subtype label (3 unchanged, 4 not available) and are excluded from the table.
- **Percentages:** they use only patients with a 7p call, so the "not available" patients are left out of the denominator.

**Statistical test:** the right test for this 3-group by gain/no-gain table is a chi-squared test, and the expected cell counts are large enough for it to be reliable. You can run it in the comparison link below or in R with `chisq.test()`. If you test several chromosome arms at once, apply a Benjamini-Hochberg correction.

[Open the arm-level copy number comparison across the three subtypes](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/generic_assay_armlevel_cna?comparisonId=6b06da6bf66565fdc7916acb)

This page ranks chromosome arms by how much their gain and loss frequencies differ across the groups. It includes the test statistics for 7p. The groups are IDH-wildtype (92 samples), IDH-mutant codeleted (167) and IDH-mutant non-codeleted (248). Those sample counts include samples without a 7p call, so they are larger than the profiled counts above.

You can also explore each group in the [study view](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018). The older TCGA Firehose Legacy LGG study (`lgg_tcga`) has no arm-level copy number data.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 30s · 647,306 tok · $0.180 · 8 LLM / 12 tool calls · trace

Yes, the counts in TCGA LGG (PanCancer Atlas) show a strong association. I can't give you a p-value, because I can't run statistical tests. The link below runs the test in cBioPortal.

**Counts (samples, mutation-profiled, with an arm-level 19q call; 440 samples)**

| | 19q loss | No 19q loss (unchanged or gain) | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CIC-mutant** | 104 (99.0%) | 1 (1.0%) | 105 |
| **CIC wild-type** | 81 (24.2%) | 254 (75.8%) | 335 |

- The 74 samples with no 19q call (3 CIC-mutant, 71 wild-type) are left out of the table.
- The cohort is 514 samples, 108 of them CIC-mutant.
- Of the 105 CIC-mutant samples with a 19q call, 104 have 19q loss. The one exception is 19q "unchanged".
- Among wild-type samples, 19 have 19q gain, 235 are unchanged, and 81 have loss.
- 19q loss is also common without a CIC mutation (81 samples), so CIC mutation isn't required for it.
- These counts show the direction and size of the association, but they don't establish significance.

**Test to run:** a 2×2 Fisher's exact test (two-tailed). One cell has a count of 1, so Fisher's is the right choice over chi-squared.
- **R:** `fisher.test(matrix(c(104, 81, 1, 254), nrow=2))`
- **Python:** `scipy.stats.fisher_exact([[104, 1], [81, 254]])`

**In cBioPortal:** [CIC-mutant vs wild-type: arm-level CNA enrichment](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/generic_assay_armlevel_cna?comparisonId=6b06da8a90693c4391916acc) shows 19q alongside the other chromosome arms, with statistics. The wild-type group includes only mutation-profiled samples. Here is the [study view for this cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018#filterJson=%7B%22genomicProfiles%22%3A%5B%5B%22mutations%22%5D%5D%7D).

The 19q call comes from the study's arm-level copy-number annotation, not from a gene-level CNA call.

EGFR mRNA expression is highest in glioblastoma and falls to near zero in leukemia.

I used the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohorts, which were batch-normalized together, so the values can be compared across cancer types. Expression is log2(RSEM + 1), counted per sample. Z-scores wouldn't work here, because they're centered within each study and hide differences between cancer types.

I've listed the top 12 cancer types by 90th percentile below. I included that percentile because EGFR amplification in a subset of tumors shows up in the upper tail, not the median.

| Cancer type | Samples | Median | 90th percentile |
|---|---|---|---|
| Glioblastoma | 160 | 11.93 | 15.00 |
| Glioma (lower grade) | 514 | 11.24 | 13.05 |
| Head and Neck Cancer | 515 | 11.50 | 12.98 |
| Renal Clear Cell Carcinoma | 510 | 11.28 | 12.54 |
| Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer | 994 | 10.49 | 12.05 |
| Esophagogastric Cancer | 593 | 9.60 | 11.70 |
| Bladder Cancer | 407 | 9.27 | 11.45 |
| Pleural Mesothelioma | 87 | 9.75 | 11.33 |
| Cervical Cancer | 294 | 9.71 | 11.32 |
| Hepatobiliary Cancer | 366 | 9.67 | 11.22 |
| Renal Non-Clear Cell Carcinoma | 348 | 9.41 | 11.03 |
| Thyroid Cancer | 498 | 9.61 | 10.96 |

- **Highest:** Glioblastoma has the highest 90th percentile (15.00). Its median (11.93) is also the highest. The gap between its median and its 90th percentile is wider than for any other cancer type in the list, so it has a high-expressing tail. The values are consistent with the amplification you'd expect in a subset of these tumors, but I didn't check copy number here.
- **Intermediate:** Breast cancer has a median of 7.39 and a 90th percentile of 9.76. Ovarian epithelial tumors have a median of 7.76 and a 90th percentile of 9.02.
- **Lowest:** Melanoma has a median of 4.85, and mature B-cell neoplasms 4.25. Leukemia has a median of −0.10, which is essentially no expression.

Sample counts are TCGA samples, and a few cohorts overlap, such as glioma and glioblastoma, so don't add the totals together.

I used the two TCGA PanCancer Atlas lung cohorts, counting samples. Each sample is counted once per mutation type, so a sample with two types appears in both rows.

| Mutation type | Samples | Distinct protein changes |
|---|---|---|
| Missense | 53 (9.4%) | 25 |
| In-frame deletion | 26 (4.6%) | 7 |
| In-frame insertion | 3 (0.5%) | 3 |
| Nonsense | 2 (0.4%) | 2 |
| Frame-shift deletion | 1 (0.2%) | 1 |
| Splice region | 1 (0.2%) | 1 |

The most common missense changes in LUAD are:
- L858R: 23 samples (4.1%)
- L861Q: 3 samples
- S768I, T790M, G719A and L62R: 2 samples each

**Lung squamous cell carcinoma (LUSC)**, 484 profiled samples

| Mutation type | Samples | Distinct protein changes |
|---|---|---|

| Splice region | 2 (0.4%) | 2 |
| Nonsense | 1 (0.2%) | 1 |

**Overall survival data (patients with survival data)**

| Group | Patients | Deaths | Alive/censored | Follow-up range (months) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IDH1-mutant only | 392 | 72 (18%) | 320 | 0 – 182 |
| EGFR-amplified only | 37 | 24 (65%) | 13 | 0.6 – 55 |
| Both | 2 | 0 | 2 | 40 – 64 |
| Neither | 82 | 29 (35%) | 53 | 0.1 – 211 |

- **Death rates:** About 65% of EGFR-amplified patients had died, against about 18% of IDH1-mutant patients. These are crude proportions, not survival estimates. They ignore follow-up time, and EGFR-amplified patients have shorter follow-up, with a maximum of about 55 months.
- **Overlap:** Two patients carry both alterations. The comparison link below counts them in both groups, so its group sizes are 395 and 39. The table counts them separately.
- **Small groups:** The EGFR-amplified group is small, so any survival estimate for it will be imprecise.

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 23s · 304,829 tok · $0.132 · 4 LLM / 7 tool calls · trace

I compared KRAS-mutant and KRAS-wild-type tumors in the TCGA PanCancer Atlas colorectal (COADREAD) and lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) cohorts. The numbers below count samples and cover mutations only. They don't include copy-number changes or fusions. For each gene I show the percent mutated in KRAS-mutant tumors, then in KRAS-wild-type tumors. Genes are ranked by the size of that difference.

I haven't run a significance test, so these are descriptive differences. The comparison links at the bottom run the significance test.

## Colorectal (COADREAD): 218 KRAS-mutant and 316 wild-type samples

| Gene | KRAS-mutant | KRAS wild-type | Difference (points) |
|---|---|---|---|
| APC | 83.5% (182) | 64.9% (205) | +18.6 |
| PIK3CA | 38.1% (83) | 20.3% (64) | +17.8 |
| PCBP1 | 9.2% (20) | 1.6% (5) | +7.6 |
| TMPRSS15 | 8.7% (19) | 2.2% (7) | +6.5 |
| BRAF | 2.8% (6) | 17.7% (56) | −14.9 |
| COL7A1 | 3.2% (7) | 12.7% (40) | −9.5 |
| ABCA13 | 10.1% (22) | 17.4% (55) | −7.3 |
| NRAS | 2.3% (5) | 8.9% (28) | −6.6 |
| ARID1A | 6.9% (15) | 13.6% (43) | −6.7 |

- **Enriched in KRAS-mutant tumors:** APC and PIK3CA are mutated more often.
- **Depleted in KRAS-mutant tumors:** BRAF and NRAS are mutated much less often. Both are in the same signaling pathway.
- **Hypermutation:** The wild-type group probably includes hypermutated (MSI) tumors. That can inflate the wild-type rates of large genes such as COL7A1, ABCA13, DOCK3 and ZNF469.

## Lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD): 168 KRAS-mutant and 398 wild-type samples

| Gene | KRAS-mutant | KRAS wild-type | Difference (points) |
|---|---|---|---|
| STK11 | 22.6% (38) | 9.3% (37) | +13.3 |
| ATM | 16.7% (28) | 4.8% (19) | +11.9 |
| MUC16 | 50.0% (84) | 39.7% (158) | +10.3 |
| CSMD1 | 28.6% (48) | 19.3% (77) | +9.3 |
| CNTNAP2 | 19.0% (32) | 9.8% (39) | +9.2 |
| RIMS2 | 19.0% (32) | 10.1% (40) | +8.9 |
| TP53 | 36.9% (62) | 58.5% (233) | −21.6 |
| EGFR | 0.6% (1) | 17.3% (69) | −16.7 |
| NF1 | 4.8% (8) | 14.6% (58) | −9.8 |
| BRAF | 1.8% (3) | 9.5% (38) | −7.7 |

- **Enriched in KRAS-mutant tumors:** STK11 and ATM are mutated more often.
- **Depleted in KRAS-mutant tumors:** TP53, EGFR, NF1 and BRAF are mutated less often. EGFR and KRAS mutations rarely occur together.
- **Likely passenger genes:** MUC16, CSMD1, CNTNAP2 and RIMS2 are large genes. Their differences may be passenger effects rather than biology.

These pages show mutation and copy-number enrichment, with significance tests, between KRAS-mutant and KRAS-wild-type samples:

- [KRAS-mutant vs. wild-type in lung adenocarcinoma (TCGA PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/comparison/alterations?cancer_study_list=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%3A+MUT&case_set_id=luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&profileFilter=mutations)

I'll start by reading the relevant guides and looking up glioma studies.In TCGA lower-grade glioma, MGMT methylation levels are higher in IDH1-mutant tumors than in IDH1 wild-type tumors. I haven't run a statistical test, so I can't say whether the difference is significant.

**Cohort:** [Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lgg_tcga). The unit is samples with both MGMT methylation (450K array) and mutation data. Methylation is a beta value, from 0 (unmethylated) to 1 (fully methylated). The values are the portal's gene-level MGMT summary, not a specific probe or the promoter alone.

| Group | Samples | Median beta (IQR) | Mean beta | Range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IDH1 mutant | 221 | 0.46 (0.31–0.58) | 0.43 | 0.015–0.80 |
| IDH1 wild-type | 65 | 0.07 (0.02–0.41) | 0.19 | 0.013–0.76 |

- **Group medians:** The IDH1-mutant median (0.46) is much higher than the wild-type median (0.07).
- **Overlap:** The ranges overlap widely, so IDH1 status doesn't determine MGMT methylation. Some wild-type tumors are highly methylated.
- **Possible confounding:** This is a lower-grade cohort, and IDH1 status is closely tied to histology and grade. Some of the difference could come from tumor subtype rather than IDH1 itself. Stratifying by subtype or grade would help.
- **Glioblastoma cross-check:** In the TCGA glioblastoma study (`gbm_tcga`, 450K array), only 6 samples are IDH1-mutant, against 110 wild-type. The medians are 0.24 for mutant and 0.08 for wild-type. That is too few mutant samples to draw conclusions.

**Testing significance:** For two groups with a continuous methylation value, the standard test in cBioPortal is a t-test. The link below runs it for you. You could also run it in R with `t.test()` or in Python with `scipy.stats.ttest_ind`.

**Why P135L can't be p14^ARF^**
- cBioPortal reports CDKN2A protein changes using one transcript, which is the p16^INK4a^ numbering. It doesn't annotate p14^ARF^ separately.
- p14^ARF^ is a shorter protein, about 132 residues. This is general background, not from cBioPortal data. Codon 135 therefore doesn't exist in p14^ARF^.
- Exon 2 changes can affect both proteins, but the residue numbers differ between them.

**What the database has at codon 135**
I found no P135L in any study. These are the proline-135 variants in CDKN2A, as sample counts per study:

Unified agent · ✓ Pass 18s · 383,327 tok · $0.085 · 6 LLM / 8 tool calls · trace

Yes. MSK-CHORD (MSK, Nature 2024) records radiation therapy as treatment events on each patient's timeline.

- **Patients with a radiation event:** 12,318 patients, with 29,158 radiation events in total. Many patients have several courses.
- **What is recorded:** Each event is labelled "Radiation Therapy" and has a start and stop time in days from diagnosis. It has no agent, dose, site or modality. You can tell who was irradiated and when, but not how.
- **Counts only:** The data can't separate "never received radiation" from "radiation not recorded". Some treatment data is also extracted from clinical notes by NLP. Treat 12,318 as the number of patients with a recorded radiation event. I haven't given a percentage of the cohort because of this.

**How to see these patients in cBioPortal**
- [Open the MSK-CHORD study summary](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024). Radiation therapy shows up in the treatment chart. If the chart isn't visible, add it from the Charts menu.
- To list individual patients, filter to the radiation group. Then click **View selected cases** to page through them, or use the Clinical Data tab to download the table. Each patient's timeline in Patient View also shows the radiation events.
